## Page 1


KOSZUL MODULES, HOLONOMY LIE ALGEBRAS, AND RESONANCE OF
GROUPS AND CDGAS
ALEXANDER I. SUCIU1 ID
Abstract. We develop a Koszul-theoretic framework for comparing classical Alexander-type invari-
ants with infinitesimal invariants arising from finite-type commutative differential graded algebra
models. The central mechanism is Koszul linearization, which replaces nonlinear equivariant con-
structions with functorial algebraic objects defined from a cdga.
To a connected cdga (A, d) with finite-dimensional H1(A) we associate Koszul modules Bi(A)
over the symmetric algebra on H1(A). We prove that the first Koszul module B1(A) is isomorphic to
the infinitesimal Alexander invariant of the holonomy Lie algebra h(A), yielding explicit formulas for
holonomy Chen ranks.
We establish a tangent cone theorem for resonance varieties, showing that cohomology controls
their first-order behavior at the origin. For finitely generated groups admitting 1-finite 1-models, we
prove that classical Alexander invariants agree with Koszul invariants after completion and passage
to associated graded objects, and that Chen ranks are determined by the model. Applications include
Chen rank computations for 2-step nilpotent Lie algebras and pure elliptic braid groups, partial for-
mality results for Sasakian manifolds, and a general framework for detecting non-formality of spaces,
groups, and maps.

Introduction
i. Basic objects and scope
We study spaces, groups, and maps via finite-type commutative differential graded algebra mod-
els, with the goal of comparing invariants defined purely from cohomology with finer, infinitesi-
mal invariants extracted from such models. The guiding theme is that cohomology captures only
first-order information at the origin of natural representation spaces, while higher-order structure
persists in finite-type models. In particular, discrepancies between cohomological invariants and
their model-theoretic analogues provide concrete and computable obstructions to formality.
The central mechanism is Koszul linearization, which replaces nonlinear or equivariant construc-
tions—such as universal abelian covers and Alexander invariants—with functorial algebraic objects
associated to a cdga. This procedure yields computable invariants that interpolate between coho-
mology and minimal models and serve as infinitesimal avatars of classical topological constructions.
Concretely, let (A, d) be a commutative differential graded algebra over a field k of characteristic
0. In most results we assume A is connected (A0 = k) with dimk H1(A) < ∞. To such a cdga we


## Page 3


KOSZUL MODULES, HOLONOMY, AND RESONANCE
3
associate Koszul chain and cochain complexes whose homology and cohomology define a family
of graded and filtered modules.
A recurring theme is the comparison between invariants computed from the cohomology alge-
bra H∗(A) and those computed from a finite-type model A. In formal situations, these invariants
necessarily coincide; discrepancies therefore quantify the failure of formality and provide refined
information invisible to cohomology alone. This philosophy applies uniformly across a wide range
of geometric contexts, including nilmanifolds, Sasakian manifolds, configuration spaces of elliptic
curves, and groups admitting 1-finite 1-models.
Resonance varieties provide the most visible instance of the phenomenon described above. For
a space or cdga A, one may compare the classical resonance varieties defined from H∗(A) with
the resonance varieties defined from a finite-type model. In many non-formal examples—most
notably nilmanifolds—these two invariants differ dramatically. Such discrepancies cannot occur in
the formal category and therefore provide effective obstructions to formality. More generally, the
tangent-cone theorem proved here shows that cohomology governs only the first-order behavior of
resonance at the origin, while higher-order structure is detected by the model.
A conceptual novelty of this approach is that it applies not only to spaces and groups, but also
to maps. Functoriality of Koszul modules detects non-formality of morphisms that act trivially on
cohomology, a phenomenon invisible to classical cohomological methods. Resonance varieties,
holonomy Lie algebras, Alexander invariants, and Chen ranks all arise naturally from this single
Koszul-theoretic construction.
Overall, the paper proposes Koszul linearization as a unifying lens through which a wide range
of infinitesimal invariants can be compared, computed, and applied. The strength of this perspective
lies in its functoriality, its compatibility with finite-type models, and its ability to translate discrep-
ancies between cohomology and models into robust algebraic and topological conclusions.
ii. Models and algebraic invariants
We work systematically with finite-type cdga models of spaces and groups, together with their
associated Koszul complexes, holonomy Lie algebras, and linearized invariants. Classical objects—
such as Alexander invariants, Malcev Lie algebras, and resonance varieties—admit infinitesimal
counterparts within this framework, related by a precise dictionary.
Topological versus infinitesimal invariants. On the topological side lie equivariant chain com-
plexes, Alexander invariants, and representation-theoretic jump loci; on the infinitesimal side lie
Koszul modules, holonomy Lie algebras, and resonance schemes defined from models. In favorable
cases these worlds coincide, while in general their divergence encodes higher-order structure.
The basic objects studied in this paper are spaces, groups, and maps, together with their algebraic
avatars arising from rational homotopy theory, Lie theory, and commutative algebra. Although
manifolds and their fundamental groups provide many of the motivating examples, our methods
and results apply more broadly to arbitrary spaces and finitely generated groups admitting finite-
type cdga models.
A central structural thread running through the paper concerns the interplay between the first
Koszul module B1(A), the holonomy Lie algebra h(A), degree-1 resonance, and Chen ranks. While
these objects have appeared separately in the literature, one of the main contributions of this work is
to show that they are governed by a single algebraic mechanism, intrinsic to finite-type cdga models.


## Page 4


4
ALEXANDER I. SUCIU
A key technical ingredient is an explicit presentation of B1(A) as a finitely presented module
over the polynomial ring S = Sym(H1(A)). This presentation depends only on the linear differential
and quadratic multiplication data of the model and extends, in a conceptually transparent way, the
foundational constructions of Papadima–Suciu in the quadratic case. It provides the algebraic bridge
between Koszul homology and the infinitesimal Alexander invariant of the holonomy Lie algebra.
Building on this presentation, we establish a natural isomorphism B1(A)  B(h(A)), placing
Chen ranks and degree-1 resonance under direct homological control. A notable feature of our
approach is that this identification is obtained by purely algebraic means, via Koszul homology
and general properties of exact complexes, rather than by dimension counting or external group-
theoretic input. As a consequence, classical results—such as the computation of Chen ranks for
free groups—emerge formally from the theory, rather than serving as foundational inputs.
From this perspective, resonance, holonomy, and Chen ranks emerge as different manifestations
of a single algebraic mechanism, here made explicit through the isomorphism B1(A)  B(h(A)).
This viewpoint clarifies the role of earlier quadratic and formal constructions and extends them to
substantially broader settings.
At a methodological level, the theory replaces nonlinear or equivariant constructions by lin-
earized, functorial invariants defined directly from models. Universal abelian covers, Alexander
invariants, and Malcev completions thus acquire Koszul-theoretic counterparts that retain first-order
information while making higher-order structure accessible to computation.
Finiteness and quadraticity. The primary focus is on spaces and groups that are typically non-
formal but admit finite-type or 1-finite models. Distinctions between quadratic, filtered, and non-
quadratic structures play a central role in determining which invariants are rigid and which detect
genuinely higher-order phenomena.
Objects fall naturally into three broad classes. Formal objects are those for which cohomology
provides a complete model and all invariants agree. At the opposite extreme are objects admitting
no finite-type models, where one is forced to work entirely at the cohomology level. The primary
focus of this paper lies in the intermediate regime: objects that are typically non-formal yet admit
finite-type cdga models. It is precisely in this last setting that the comparison between classical and
infinitesimal invariants becomes meaningful and effective.
A further organizing principle concerns quadraticity. At the level of Lie algebras, one may dis-
tinguish between quadratic, homogeneous, and non-homogeneous structures; at the level of cdgas,
between formal, partially formal, and non-formal objects. These distinctions are reflected in the be-
havior of Koszul homology, holonomy Lie algebras, and resonance varieties, and they play a central
role in the applications developed later in the paper.
Table 1 summarizes the main parallel objects considered throughout this work, arranged so as
to emphasize the dictionary between topological, rational-homotopical, Lie-theoretic, and algebraic
invariants. Each block of the table corresponds to a major thematic component of the paper and
serves as a guide to the structure of the next several subsections.
Having outlined the two parallel worlds we study, we now turn to the main results connecting
them, organized by theme. The results establish precise correspondences between classical invari-
ants arising from abelian covers and their infinitesimal counterparts from finite-type cdga models,
and may be grouped into five interrelated themes: Koszul linearization and spectral sequences; holo-
nomy Lie algebras and the first Koszul module; topological linearization and Alexander invariants;
algebraic models, Chen ranks, and nilpotent Lie algebras; and resonance varieties.

iii. Main themes and results
The main results are organized around a functorial Koszul mechanism linking commutative dif-
ferential graded algebra, Lie theory, algebraic topology, and group theory. They fall into five inter-
related themes.
Koszul linearization and spectral sequences. At the foundation of the paper lies a systematic
study of Koszul (co)homology associated to finite-type cdga models. Let (A, d) be a connected k-
cdga with H1(A) finite-dimensional. Set S = Sym(H1(A)), where H1(A) denotes the dual of H1(A),
and let ωA ∈H1(A) ⊗k H1(A) be the canonical element (a cocycle, since d is zero on degree-0
elements). The Koszul chain complex of A is the S -linear complex

where ωA acts as an S -linear derivation of A ⊗k S via contraction on the A-factor. Its homology
modules, Bi(A) B Hi(K•(A)), are called the Koszul modules of A. All of these constructions are
functorial with respect to cdga morphisms, a feature that becomes essential in later comparisons
with topological and group-theoretic invariants.
Koszul linearization leads to functorial spectral sequences that encode higher-order algebraic data
in a controlled way. Our first main result establishes the existence and structure of these Koszul
spectral sequences. In both homological and cohomological form, they are shown to be compatible
with natural comodule or algebra structures, with differentials expressed in terms of iterated left
cup-products (or contraction) by the canonical class ωA.

The higher differentials are expressed in terms of iterated left cup-products (or contractions) by the
canonical element ωA. In particular, both spectral sequences degenerate at E2 if A is formal.
The cohomological spectral sequence arises from the m-adic filtration on K•(A) and lives in the
first quadrant, while the homological spectral sequence arises from the n-adic filtration on K•(A)
and lives in the second quadrant (p ≤0 in the natural indexing). Precise multiplicative and comul-
tiplicative refinements are established in Theorems 5.1 and 5.2.
The naturality of these constructions is developed in Section 8: Koszul modules are contravari-
antly functorial in general, but become covariantly functorial—and preserve algebra and coalgebra
structures (Theorem 8.9)—under morphisms inducing isomorphisms on H1. A striking illustra-
tion is that a quasi-isomorphism V(a) →CE(sol2) shifts the support of B1 away from the origin,
showing that Koszul homology detects cochain-level data invisible to cohomology (Example 8.3).
When A admits positive weights, the m-adic filtration on K•(A) is refined by the total-weight fil-
tration of Section 7.2. As shown in Theorem 7.5, the associated weight spectral sequence identifies
at the E1-page with the Koszul chain complex of the cohomology algebra H∗(A) (with zero differ-
ential), and converges strongly and unconditionally to the weight-graded Koszul homology of A.
Higher differentials in this spectral sequence measure the deviation of A from formality within each
weight, and their vanishing in low total degrees provides effective obstructions to partial formality;
see Section 17.4. Thus, for positive-weight models, the first nontrivial page is determined entirely
by H∗(A), while the filtration on Koszul homology admits a natural multigraded description.
Holonomy Lie algebras and the first Koszul module. We now strengthen the finiteness hypoth-
esis slightly: we assume dimk A1 < ∞(rather than merely dimk H1(A) < ∞), as the holonomy Lie
algebra h(A) is constructed directly from A1. Associated to A is its holonomy Lie algebra h(A),
defined as the quotient of the free Lie algebra on A1 = (A1)∨by the ideal generated by the im-
age of the dual multiplication map µ∨: (A2)∨→V2 A1 and by the image of the dual differential
d∨: (A2)∨→A1. This is a finitely presented Lie algebra whose relations encode both the cup
product structure and the linear part of the differential of A.
A second main result (proved in Theorem 14.7) is a substantial generalization of [79, Prop. 9.3].
It identifies the first Koszul homology module B1(A) with B(h(A)) B h′(A)/h′′(A), the infinitesimal
Alexander invariant of the holonomy Lie algebra.

a fact proved in Proposition 14.12. This identity provides an effective method for computing holo-

From this perspective, B1(A) isolates the quadratic core of the Koszul theory: although higher
Koszul homology detects non-formal and genuinely higher-order phenomena, the first Koszul mod-
ule is entirely determined by holonomy. As a result, invariants extracted from B1(A)—such as
resonance support loci and holonomy Chen ranks—are controlled by the quadratic data encoded
in h(A), even when the differential of A is not itself quadratic. At the same time, the isomor-
phism B1(A)  B(h(A)) is an identification of invariants of a fixed cdga; under morphisms, the two
sides behave differently. The induced map h(φ): h( ¯A) →h(A) depends only on φ1 : A1 →¯A1 and
φ2 : A2 →¯A2, while B1(φ) depends on all of φ, making B1 sensitive to cochain-level data invisible
to h (see Remark 14.9 and Example 14.10 for an illustration). This sensitivity is exploited in Sec-
tion 20, where the induced map on Koszul modules furnishes an obstruction to 1-formality of group
homomorphisms even when both source and target are individually 1-formal (Proposition 20.6).
Together, Theorems A and B form the algebraic backbone of the paper: they show that resonance,
holonomy, and infinitesimal Alexander invariants are governed by a single Koszul mechanism in-
trinsic to finite-type cdga models.
Topological linearization and Alexander invariants. The algebraic theory developed above inter-
acts functorially with the topology of universal abelian covers. Let X be a connected CW-complex
with fundamental group G = π1(X), and set Λ = k[Gab], I ⊂Λ the augmentation ideal, and
S = Sym(H1(X; k)). The Alexander invariants Bi(X; k) B Hi(Xab; k), viewed as Λ-modules, encode
deep information about the topology of X but are often difficult to compute directly. A key role in
this paper is played by the identification of the equivariant chain complexes of the universal abelian
cover with Koszul complexes, via a linearization process.
At the E1-level, the equivariant spectral sequence associated to the I-adic filtration on C•(Xab; k)
is canonically identified with the Koszul cochain complex K•(H∗(X; k)), for any finite-type space
X (Theorem 15.3). Under an additional minimality hypothesis on the CW-structure of X, this iden-
tification upgrades to an isomorphism of associated graded cochain complexes grI C•(Xab; k) 
K•(H∗(X; k)), and under further hypotheses—the existence of a finite-type cdga model with pos-
itive weights—it upgrades further to a filtered quasi-isomorphism of completions, recovering the
Alexander invariants themselves from Koszul homology.
Theorem C. Let X be a connected, finite-type, minimal CW-complex. Set Λ = k[Gab] with augmen-

Algebraic models for spaces and groups. The Koszul-theoretic framework developed here builds
on the rational homotopy theories of Quillen [93] and Sullivan [113], emphasizing the non-simply
connected setting where the fundamental group and its associated algebraic invariants play a cen-
tral role. In contrast with the simply connected case—where minimal models are concentrated in
degrees ≥2—the presence of nontrivial π1 introduces degree-one generators, filtered Lie algebras,
and infinitesimal invariants associated to abelian covers. From a homological algebra viewpoint,
Koszul complexes and BGG duality provide the mechanism for linearizing this additional structure,
in the spirit of the Avramov–Halperin correspondence between rational homotopy theory and local
algebra [6].
Given a 1-finite 1-model (A, d) for a finitely generated group G, the holonomy Lie algebra h(A)
is a filtered Lie algebra attached to A, refining the quadratic holonomy Lie algebra h(H∗(A)), which
depends only on the cup product. By [88], the Malcev Lie algebra m(G; k) identifies with the lower
central series completion of h(A). Thus h(A) encodes the full pronilpotent completion of G, while
formality governs whether this completion is determined by the quadratic cohomology algebra.
Koszul modules provide a bridge between these Lie-theoretic invariants and algebraic models:
discrepancies between h(A) and h(H∗(A))—that is, failures of formality—are reflected in Koszul

homology and Chen ranks. This viewpoint isolates precisely when quadratic data suffice and when
higher-order structure is present.
Although phrased in terms of groups, the framework applies equally to spaces admitting finite-
type cdga models. Examples include compact nilmanifolds, configuration spaces of complex curves,
and Sasakian manifolds. In these settings, holonomy Lie algebras and Koszul invariants capture
geometric information not visible at the level of cohomology.
Alexander invariants, Koszul modules, and Chen ranks. Another organizing theme is the com-
parison between classical Alexander invariants of groups and their Koszul-theoretic counterparts
arising from algebraic models.
For a finitely generated group G, the Alexander invariant B1(G; k) is the k[Gab]-module H1(G′; k),
equivalently the first homology of the universal abelian cover of a K(G, 1). Given a 1-finite 1-model
(A, d), one associates the first Koszul module B1(A), defined as the degree-one homology of the
Koszul complex of A.
For 1-formal groups, the completed Alexander invariant is determined by the holonomy Lie al-
gebra [79, 37]. Theorem 19.2 removes the formality hypothesis and places this correspondence in a
uniform cdga-theoretic framework.
Theorem D. Let G be a finitely generated group. Suppose G admits a 1-finite 1-model (A, d) over
a field k of characteristic 0. Then:
(1) The completed Alexander invariant of G is isomorphic to the completed first Koszul module

This result shows that Chen ranks are infinitesimal invariants: although defined at the level of
the group, they are completely determined by the Koszul complex of any 1-finite 1-model. The
identification of the generating series of Chen ranks with the Hilbert series of gr(B1(A)) is the
mechanism behind their connection with resonance varieties and eventual vanishing phenomena
developed later in the paper.
In Section 6, we further identify the graded pieces of gr Bi(A) with graded Tor-groups over the
exterior algebra via BGG duality (Theorem 6.4). In particular, Corollary 6.6 shows that Chen ranks
exhibit rigid homological behavior: once they vanish in sufficiently high degree, they remain zero
thereafter. Thus Chen ranks either persist indefinitely or vanish eventually.

ALEXANDER I. SUCIU
Nilpotent Lie algebras, CE-models, and Chen ranks. For finitely generated torsion-free nilpo-
tent groups G, the Chevalley–Eilenberg cdga A = CE(g) of the Malcev Lie algebra g provides a
canonical 1-finite 1-model, so that Theorem D applies and gives θn(G) = θn(A) for all n ≥1. The
Koszul module B1(A) thus carries complete information about the Chen ranks of G, reducing an
intrinsically group-theoretic problem to a computation in commutative algebra over the polynomial
ring S = Sym(H1(G; k)).
This reduction is especially effective for 2-step nilpotent Lie algebras. For such an algebra h,
the Chevalley–Eilenberg cdga A = CE(h) carries a natural positive-weight decomposition (Proposi-
tion 11.5), making B1(A) a graded S -module with an explicit presentation (Proposition 14.15). The
free 2-step nilpotent Lie algebra fm,2 = fm/γ3fm on m generators plays a universal role: every 2-step
nilpotent h with dim h1 = m is a quotient of fm,2, inducing a surjection of Koszul modules and hence
universal upper bounds for Chen ranks. The following result, proved in Theorems 14.16 and 14.19,
makes all of this explicit.
Theorem E. Let h be a finite-dimensional 2-step nilpotent Lie algebra over k, with m = dim h1.
(1) (Free case.) For the free 2-step nilpotent Lie algebra fm,2 on m ≥2 generators, there is an

Resonance varieties, cohomology jump loci, and tangent cones. Resonance varieties furnish the
most concrete manifestation of the comparison between cohomology-level and model-level invari-
ants developed here. These varieties also connect to the broader theory of cohomology jump loci,
capturing the tangent-cone approximation to characteristic varieties.

Applying these constructions to H∗(A) (with d = 0) yields the cohomology-level resonance va-
rieties Ri,s(H∗(A)) and Ri,s(H∗(A)). In formal situations, model-level and cohomology-level reso-
nance coincide; discrepancies detect non-formality.
Our results fall into two complementary parts. First, we prove a general tangent-cone theo-
rem showing that cohomology-level resonance controls only the lowest-order approximation at the
origin. Second, under positive-weight hypotheses, we establish strong global constraints on model-
level resonance, including conicality properties.
Theorem F. Let (A, d) be a connected q-finite cdga over an algebraically closed field k of charac-
teristic 0. Then for all i ≤q and s ≥1,
(i) TC0
 Ri,s(A) ⊆Ri,s(H∗(A)),
(ii) TC0
 Ri,s(A) ⊆Ri,s(H∗(A)).
If moreover A is q-formal, then equality holds in both cases.
The inclusions are proved in Theorem 9.13, by identifying the linearized Koszul differential
with the Koszul complex of H∗(A) via the first page of the Koszul spectral sequence. The equal-
ity under q-formality is Theorem 9.17, whose proof combines the inclusion with the naturality of
Koszul modules under q-quasi-isomorphisms (Theorem 8.6(2)): the q-formality zig-zag connects A
to (H∗(A), 0) by a sequence of q-quasi-isomorphisms, each preserving the associated graded Koszul
modules, forcing equality of tangent cones. This result refines earlier topological statements of
[35, 65], answers a question posed in [101], and complements the work of Budur and Rubi´o [16].
It shows that the initial homogeneous part of model-level resonance is governed by cohomology,
while higher-order structure depends on the full cdga model. In later sections, we show how fail-
ures of equality in the inclusion provide effective obstructions to formality and 1-formality. For
instance, for the Heisenberg nilmanifold (Example 9.27), the model-level resonance is trivial while
cohomology-level resonance fills all of H1, demonstrating how cohomology alone misses essen-
tial infinitesimal structure. It remains an open question whether, under q-formality, the irreducible
components of Ri,s(A) are always linear subspaces of H1(A) defined over Q (Conjecture 9.18).
Positive weights provide a particularly transparent framework for understanding resonance in
finite-type models. If (A, d) admits a positive weight decomposition compatible with the differen-
tial, the Koszul modules become genuinely graded rather than merely filtered. In this multigraded
setting, the resonance loci Ri,s(A) often decompose as finite unions of linear subspaces in H1(A),
reflecting strong rigidity imposed by weight considerations. In particular, Theorem 9.21 shows that


## Page 12

ALEXANDER I. SUCIU
for positive-weight models all resonance and support loci are conical and hence, by Theorem F,
contained in the corresponding loci of H∗(A).
The tangent cone inclusion provides an algebraic foundation for the topological theory of coho-
mology jump loci. For a space X, the main loci of interest are: the characteristic varieties V i,s(X; k),
defined from rank-one local systems; the Alexander varieties Vi,s(X; k) = Supp(Vs Bi(X; k)), de-
fined from Alexander invariants; and the resonance varieties Ri,s(X; k) = Ri,s(H∗(X; k)) and sup-
port loci Ri,s(X; k), defined from cohomology.
Libgober proved that the tangent cone at the identity to V i,s(X; C) is included in Ri,s(X; C).
Dimca–Papadima–Suciu and Dimca–Papadima showed that for q-formal spaces, the analytic germs
of V i,s(X; C) at the identity and Ri,s(X; C) at the origin coincide for i ≤q. Theorem F supplies
the algebraic mechanism underlying this topological inclusion. When X admits a finite-type model
(A, d) over C, the analytic germ of V i,s(X; C) at the identity is identified with the germ of Ri,s(A) at
the origin. The containment TC0(Ri,s(A)) ⊆Ri,s(H∗(A)) then shows that cohomology governs the
lowest-order infinitesimal structure of resonance, a conclusion that extends to any i-finite model by
a finite-dimensional approximation theorem for resonance data (Theorem 9.6). Higher-order terms
depend on the full cdga model.
Chen ranks and resonance. The preceding results establish that Chen ranks are determined by
Koszul homology. We now place this in the broader context of the relationship between Chen ranks
and resonance varieties, a theme running throughout the paper. Our results clarify the infinitesimal
and homotopy-theoretic foundations of this correspondence, showing that the Koszul linearization
process provides a natural mechanism for approximating higher-order group invariants by linear
data. Within this framework, phenomena such as eventual vanishing of Chen ranks, additivity
properties, and asymptotic formulas reflect precise features of the associated resonance schemes.
The structural conditions governing when Chen ranks are controlled by resonance—isotropicity and
separability of resonance components—are introduced in Section 19.4 and applied in Section 19.5.
At the same time, this perspective delineates the scope of purely infinitesimal models: finer
scheme-theoretic properties—such as separability of resonance components or reducedness—typi-
cally require additional geometric or algebro-geometric input beyond finite-type cdga models alone.
Rather than constituting an obstruction, this distinction highlights the role of Koszul invariants as a
first-order approximation, one that is often sharp and, in many geometric settings, linear or reduced,
as illustrated by numerous examples throughout the paper. In the absence of these conditions, the
formula fails: right-angled Artin groups and upper McCool groups provide explicit counterexam-
ples (Remark 19.15).
Under suitable geometric hypotheses, this approximation becomes effective and even optimal,
leading to precise asymptotic formulas for Chen ranks in terms of resonance; see, for instance, [1, 2,
3, 4] and the survey [46]. Together, these results suggest that Koszul-theoretic methods form a robust
bridge between homotopy-theoretic invariants and algebraic geometry, with substantial potential for
further development. The extension of these ideas beyond the 1-formal setting, to groups admitting
positive-weight cdga models with linear resonance, is the subject of Question 19.17.
Geometric applications: positive-weight models beyond the CE setting. The Koszul module
techniques developed in this paper apply to any k-cdga model carrying a positive-weight decom-
position, regardless of whether that model arises as a Chevalley–Eilenberg complex. Two classes
of examples illustrate this generality: configuration spaces on elliptic curves, where the model is


## Page 13

13
of Morgan–Gysin type, and Sasakian manifolds and Seifert fibered spaces, where the model is a
Hirsch extension of a formal base.
Configuration spaces on elliptic curves. The pure elliptic braid groups P1,n = π1(Conf(E, n))
are non-1-formal, yet admit an explicit finite-type k-cdga model A(n) due to Bibby [10], carrying
a positive-weight structure inherited from Deligne’s mixed Hodge theory on quasi-projective vari-
eties. The model A(n) is of Morgan–Gysin type: its generators in degree 1 consist of the torus classes
ai, bi (weight 1) and the hyperplane classes ei j (weight 2), with differential dei j = (ai −aj)(bi −bj).
Despite the non-formality and non-CE nature of this model, the Koszul module B1(A(n)) decom-
poses completely: with S = k[x1, y1, . . . , xn, yn],

Sasakian manifolds and Seifert fibered spaces. A compact Sasakian manifold M2n+1 carries a
canonical S 1-action with orbit space a compact K¨ahler orbifold B. The Tievsky model [115]

realizes M as a 1-step Hirsch extension of the formal cdga (H∗(B; k), 0) and carries a natural
positive-weight structure. The Koszul–Gysin exact sequence associated to such extensions (The-
orem 10.6) provides effective control of formality and Massey products.
A key result (Theorem 22.11) states that a 1-step Hirsch extension of a formal, finite-type,
positive-weight cdga of cohomological dimension 2n is (n−1)-formal; if the Euler class is nonzero,
it fails to be n-formal and admits a nontrivial triple Massey product as obstruction. Applied to the
Tievsky model, this recovers the partial formality of Sasakian manifolds established in [88, 54] by
purely algebraic means. All Massey products of length ≥4 vanish (Theorem 22.2), so the triple
Massey product detects the first non-formal behavior. For orientable Seifert fibered spaces M →Σg
with g > 0, the same Hirsch extension framework yields filtered formality of π1(M) via Proposi-
tion 18.8; see Section 22.5.
iv. Organization of the paper
This monograph is organized into four parts, progressing from algebraic foundations to applica-
tions in topology and geometry.
Part I: Algebraic foundations. We develop the Koszul-theoretic framework underlying the entire
work. After reviewing background from commutative algebra and the Bernstein–Gelfand–Gelfand
correspondence, we introduce finite-type cdgas and construct the Koszul chain and cochain com-
plexes associated to a connected cdga (A, d) with finite-dimensional H1(A). We analyze duality
properties, establish spectral sequences comparing Koszul modules of A and of H∗(A), and relate
associated graded Koszul homology to Tor groups via BGG duality. This part provides the homolog-
ical and spectral tools used in all subsequent comparison theorems. We also develop foundational


## Page 14


14
ALEXANDER I. SUCIU
commutative algebra tools, including the identification of tangent cones to support varieties via
associated graded modules, used throughout Part III.
Part II: Structural properties. We investigate additional structures that refine the Koszul frame-
work. Positive weight decompositions are introduced and shown to promote Koszul modules from
filtered to genuinely graded objects, imposing strong rigidity on resonance and support loci. For
spaces whose Sullivan minimal model carries positive weights—most notably, nilmanifolds and
quasi-projective varieties—the weight spectral sequence on the Koszul complex provides a com-
putable obstruction to partial formality, with higher differentials encoding deviations from the co-
homology algebra. We also study functoriality of Koszul modules under cdga morphisms, showing
that Koszul homology can detect non-formality of maps, even when they induce the identity on
cohomology.
Part III: Resonance and holonomy. This part develops the theory of resonance varieties and ho-
lonomy Lie algebras and connects them systematically to Koszul modules. We define resonance
jump loci Ri,s and support loci Ri,s, establish their determinantal scheme structures, and prove a
finite-dimensional approximation theorem allowing all resonance results to be stated under minimal
finiteness hypotheses. We prove the tangent cone theorem, showing that cohomology controls only
first-order behavior at the origin, and establish the equality under q-formality. We analyze reso-
nance under standard algebraic constructions and obtain vanishing results for Chevalley–Eilenberg
complexes of nilpotent Lie algebras. This part also establishes the identification B1(A)  B(h(A))
and derives the corresponding holonomy Chen rank formulas.
Part IV: Spaces, groups, and applications. We apply the algebraic theory to topology and group
theory. After revisiting equivariant spectral sequences and Alexander invariants, we establish our
main comparison theorem for finitely generated groups admitting 1-finite 1-models, identifying
completed and graded Alexander invariants with Koszul modules and equating Chen ranks. This
completes the comparison theorems stated in the Introduction for finitely generated groups admit-
ting 1-finite 1-models. We also study formality of maps via functorial models. The monograph
concludes with two classes of geometric applications. For configuration spaces on elliptic curves,
we compute the Koszul module of Bibby’s positive-weight model and obtain closed-form Chen rank
formulas for the pure elliptic braid groups P1,n, providing the first non-formal instances of the Chen
ranks pattern established in the 1-formal setting. For Sasakian manifolds and Seifert fibered spaces,
we apply the Hirsch extension framework to derive partial formality results and sharp Massey prod-
uct vanishing bounds via the Koszul–Gysin long exact sequence.
The four parts are designed to be read sequentially, though readers interested primarily in appli-
cations may begin with Part IV and refer back as needed. The Koszul spectral sequences and weight
decompositions underlie all formality obstructions, while the structure of B1(A) is central to both
the holonomy and Alexander comparisons.
General background. The material in this monograph draws on several foundational areas. For
rational homotopy theory, including Sullivan models, formality, and applications to topology, we
refer to the standard sources [47, 114, 48]. Homological algebra, derived functors, and spectral
sequences are treated in [117, 53, 72], while group cohomology and homological methods in group
theory are covered in [15]. For commutative algebra, including graded algebras, resolutions, and


## Page 15


KOSZUL MODULES, HOLONOMY, AND RESONANCE
15
homological techniques, we rely on [40]. These references provide general context and terminology;
more specialized sources are cited at the point of use.
Notation. Throughout this work, k will denote a ground field of characteristic 0. Unless otherwise
specified, all tensor products, symmetric algebras, and exterior algebras will be over k. We will
often write ⊗for ⊗k when the context is clear, though we retain the subscript in definitions and
key formulas for precision. We use the notation ()∨to denote the k-linear dual of a vector space
or the dual of a k-linear map. For instance, if V is a k-vector space, then V∨= Homk(V, k), and
if φ: V →W is a k-linear map, then φ∨: W∨→V∨is the dual map. We use the notation ()∗or
()• for graded pieces of graded objects (e.g., A∗or V•) and for induced morphisms in cohomology
(e.g., f ∗: H∗(Y) →H∗(X)). When discussing graded modules, we use subscripts for homological
grading (e.g., Mi) and superscripts for cohomological grading (e.g., Mi), unless otherwise specified.
When discussing graded algebras like the symmetric algebra S = Sym(V) or the exterior algebra
E = V W, we use S k = Symk(V) and Ek = Vk W to denote their degree-k pieces (symmetric and
exterior powers, respectively). For a ring R, we write Rr to denote the free R-module of rank r. All
(co)homology groups, Alexander invariants, and Koszul modules are taken with coefficients in a
fixed field k, unless explicitly indicated otherwise. When the coefficient field plays a role, we write
Bi(X; k) and Bi(X; k).
Part I. Algebraic foundations
In this part we develop the algebraic framework underlying the entire paper. Starting from con-
nected, finite-type commutative differential graded algebras, we introduce a collection of homolog-
ical constructions—Koszul complexes, Koszul modules, and their associated spectral sequences—
that encode subtle information beyond cohomology.
A recurring theme is the systematic use of duality. Linear duality, Poincar´e duality, and filtration-
induced dualities interact to constrain the structure of Koszul (co)homology and to produce invari-
ants that admit both algebraic and geometric interpretations. Tools such as the BGG correspondence
and homological support varieties allow these invariants to be detected and organized effectively.
The results of this part provide the algebraic foundations for the study of resonance varieties,
holonomy Lie algebras, and Alexander-type invariants in later parts of the paper, where these con-
structions are applied to spaces, groups, and their infinitesimal models.
1. The BGG correspondence, supports, and jump loci
We review here several algebraic constructions that underlie much of the analysis in this paper:
the Bernstein–Gelfand–Gelfand correspondence, the notion of support for graded modules and com-
plexes, and the resulting homological support and jump loci. Together, these constructions identify
homology jump loci with determinantal and support conditions, allowing resonance varieties to be
computed scheme-theoretically via dual complexes. This algebraic framework serves as the foun-
dation for the study of resonance varieties, Koszul complexes, and their topological counterparts
developed in the sections that follow.
1.1. The BGG correspondence. Let V be a finite-dimensional k-vector space, and let V∨be the
dual k-vector space. The Bernstein–Gelfand–Gelfand correspondence is an explicit equivalence of
the bounded derived categories of graded modules over the exterior algebra E = VV∨and over
the symmetric algebra S = Sym(V); see [41]. In this work, we use the BGG correspondence in


## Page 16


16
ALEXANDER I. SUCIU
the form developed by Eisenbud–Fløystad–Schreyer [42], which realizes this equivalence via linear
free resolutions and provides a natural bridge between homological invariants and geometric support
conditions. We assign the nonzero elements of V degree 1, so that S 1 = V, while E−1 = E1 = V∨
(in degree −1).
Remark 1.1. The exterior algebra E = V V∨in this section is negatively graded: its generators V∨
sit in degree −1, so that E1 = V∨in the cohomological convention. This is the natural grading for
BGG duality, which relates positively graded S -modules to negatively graded E-modules. When
exterior algebras appear later in the paper in the context of Koszul complexes associated to a cdga
(A, d), they will carry a positive grading following the cohomological convention on H1(A); the
distinction will be clear from context.

1.2. Supports, annihilators, and tangent cones. Let R be a commutative ring, and let M be an
R-module. The support of M, denoted Supp(M), is the set of maximal ideals m ⊂R for which the
localization Mm is nonzero. Supports behave additively: if 0 →M →N →P →0 is an exact
sequence of R-modules, then Supp(N) = Supp(M) ∪Supp(P). The annihilator ideal AnnR(M) =
{ f ∈R | f M = 0} encodes the same information in the finitely generated case. The following lemma
is well-known; see e.g. [41].
Lemma 1.2. If M is a finitely generated R-module, then:
(1) Supp(M) = V(AnnR(M));
(2) V  AnnR(VkM) = V(Fittk(M)).

generated S -module M, the support Supp(M) = V(AnnS (M)) is a Zariski-closed subset of V, with
scheme structure defined by the ideal AnnS (M).

S -module equipped with its m-adic filtration. Then:
SuppS (grm M) = SuppS (M),
and

Proof. Since M is graded, AnnS (M) is a homogeneous ideal, so SuppS (M) = V(AnnS (M)) is a con-

Since SuppS (M) is already conical, TC0(SuppS M) = SuppS (M), and the two equalities follow.

1.5. Jump loci, support loci, and scheme structures. In this subsection, we clarify the relation-
ship between several a priori distinct ways of defining resonance varieties for complexes of free
modules over a polynomial ring. These include homological jump loci, cohomological jump loci
of dual complexes, and support loci of homology modules. Under mild finiteness assumptions, we
show that all these constructions determine the same closed subschemes, although their scheme-
theoretic presentations may differ.
As shown in Corollary 1.11, the homology jump loci in degree i may be computed from the
dual of the truncated complex C≤i+1. Under suitable finiteness hypotheses, this observation leads to
explicit determinantal equations for Ri,s(C).

Remark 1.17. Even when defined over the same affine space, resonance varieties admit distinct
constructions and may differ both scheme-theoretically and set-theoretically.
The homological resonance varieties Ri,s(C) are defined by determinantal ideals arising from
the differentials of the complex (Proposition 1.14), while the support loci Ri,s(C) are defined by
annihilator ideals of exterior powers of homology modules (Definition 1.6). Theorem 1.16 identifies
the jump loci Ri,s(C) with the supports of exterior powers of the cohomology of the dual complex
C#.
In general, the support loci Ri,s(C) need not coincide with the jump loci Ri,s(C), even as sets.
These two constructions agree only in a filtered sense, as explained in Theorem 1.19, and may differ
in fixed degree, as shown in Example 9.10.
Remark 1.18. The preceding results apply to arbitrary chain complexes of free modules and belong
to the general BGG framework. In later sections, we specialize to Koszul complexes associated
to cdgas, where additional structure—such as functoriality, support loci, and duality—allows for
stronger conclusions.

1.7. Propagation of jump loci. The following propagation property of jump loci was established
in [33, Thm. 1.2]; for the reader’s convenience, we include a short, self-contained proof adapted
to the present algebraic framework. Historically, this phenomenon was motivated by earlier work
of Eisenbud, Popescu, and Yuzvinsky [43], who observed propagation of (depth-1) resonance for
complements of complex hyperplane arrangements as a consequence of a linearity property of the
cohomology ring under the BGG correspondence.

2. Differential graded algebras
In this section we recall the basic notions and conventions concerning commutative differen-
tial graded algebras (cdgas), which serve as the primary algebraic framework for the constructions
developed in this paper. Working in the category of connected, finite-type cdgas allows us to for-
mulate Koszul modules, resonance varieties, and holonomy Lie algebras in a uniform and functorial
manner, while keeping close contact with the cohomology algebras of spaces and groups.


## Page 24


24
ALEXANDER I. SUCIU
2.1. Commutative differential graded algebras. We shall fix throughout a coefficient field k of
characteristic 0. A graded k-vector space is a vector space V over k, together with a direct sum de-
composition, V =
L
n≥0 Vn, into vector subspaces. An element a ∈Vn is said to be homogeneous;
we write |a| = n for its degree.
A graded k-algebra is a graded k-vector space, A• =
L
n≥0 An, equipped with an associative
multiplication map, ·: A × A →A, making A into a k-algebra with unit 1 ∈A0 such that |a · b| =
|a| + |b| for all homogenous elements a, b ∈A. A graded algebra A is said to be graded-commutative
(for short, a cga), if a · b = (−1)|a||b|b · a for all homogeneous a, b ∈A. A basic example is the
free cga on a graded k-vector space V•; this algebra, denoted by ΛV, is the tensor product of the
symmetric algebra on
L
n≥0 V2n with the exterior algebra on
L
n≥0 V2n+1.
A graded k-algebra A is connected if A0 is the k-span of the unit 1 (and thus A0 = k). We say
that A is of finite-type if all the graded pieces An are finite-dimensional, and A is q-finite (for some
integer q ≥1) if dimk An < ∞for n ≤q. A morphism between two graded algebras is a k-linear
map φ: A →B that preserves gradings and satisfies φ(a · b) = φ(a) · φ(b) for all a, b ∈A. We say φ
is a q-isomorphism if φi : Ai →Bi is an isomorphism for all i ≤q.
A commutative differential graded algebra (for short, a cdga) over a field k is a k-cga, A•,
equipped with a differential d: A →A of degree 1 satisfying the graded Leibniz rule: d(ab) =
d(a) · b + (−1)|a|a · d(b) for all homogeneous a, b ∈A. The cohomology of the underlying cochain
complex, H∗(A), inherits the structure of a cga; we will let bi(A) = dimk Hi(A) be its Betti numbers.
If A is q-finite, then Hi(A) is a subquotient of Ai for each i, and so bi(A) < ∞for all i ≤q; in
particular, the cohomology algebra H∗(A) is then q-finite as well.
A morphism between two cdgas, φ: A →B, is both an algebra map and a cochain map. Conse-
quently, φ induces a morphism, φ∗: H∗(A) →H∗(B), between the respective cohomology algebras.
We say that φ is a quasi-isomorphism if φ∗is an isomorphism. Likewise, we say φ is a q-quasi-
isomorphism (for some q ≥1) if φ∗is an isomorphism in degrees up to q and a monomorphism
in degree q + 1. Note that, unlike a q-isomorphism of cgas, the q-quasi-isomorphism condition for
cdgas includes a monomorphism requirement in degree q + 1, needed to control cohomology at the
boundary degree.
Two cdgas (A, dA) and (B, dB) are weakly equivalent (or only q-equivalent) if there is a finite
zig-zag of quasi-isomorphisms (or q-quasi-isomorphisms) connecting A to A,
(2.1)

· · ·
Aℓ
B.
In this case, we write A ≃B (or A ≃q B).
A cdga (A, dA) is said to be formal (or just q-formal) if it is weakly equivalent (or just q-
equivalent) to its cohomology algebra equipped with the zero differential, (H∗(A), 0). Formality
behaves well under field extensions. The classical result—that a connected, finite-type cdga over
k is formal if and only if its scalar extension to any field K ⊃k of characteristic 0 is formal—was
established independently by Sullivan [113], Neisendorfer–Miller [74], and Halperin–Stasheff [51].
This was generalized in [110, Thm. 4.19] to partial formality: under the assumption that H≤q+1(A)
is finite-dimensional, the cdga (A, dA) is q-formal over k if and only if (A⊗k K, dA ⊗idK) is q-formal
over K. This generalization is used throughout the paper, particularly when passing between Q, R,
C, and an arbitrary field k of characteristic 0 in formality arguments.

A cdga morphism φ: A →B is said to be formal if there is a diagram of the form (2.2) connecting

Remark 2.1. We work exclusively with commutative differential graded algebras throughout this
monograph. While formality can be defined for non-commutative dgas, and Saleh [95] showed that
a cdga is formal as a dga if and only if it is formal as a cdga—a result generalized by Campos–
Petersen–Robert-Nicoud–Wierstra [20], who proved that weak equivalence of cdgas is independent
of whether one computes in the dga or cdga category—the Koszul complex construction underlying
our theory is fundamentally geometric and requires commutativity.
Specifically, the verification that δ2

eiej = −ejei for cocycles ei, ej ∈Z1(A) of degree 1. Without graded commutativity, the required
cancellations fail and δ2

eralizations involving Hochschild homology or A∞-structures exist, they represent fundamentally
different theories that lie beyond the scope of classical rational homotopy theory as developed by
Sullivan and Quillen.
2.3. Massey products. A well known obstruction to formality is provided by the higher-order
Massey products, introduced by W.S. Massey in [69], and studied for instance in [58, 71]. Let
(A, d) be a k-dga and let u1, . . . , un be elements in H∗(A); without loss of generality, we may assume

dai, j = P

system, or, equivalently, ⟨u1, . . . , un⟩, ∅, in which case the indeterminacy of the Massey product
is the subset {u −v | u, v ∈⟨u1, . . . , un⟩} ⊆H∗(A). When a Massey product is defined, we say it
vanishes if it contains the element 0; otherwise, it is non-vanishing.
The simplest Massey triple products are as follows. Let u1, u2, u3 be elements in H1(A) such
that u1u2 = u2u3 = 0. We may then choose 1-cocycles a0,1, a1,2, a2,3 representing u1, u2, u3 and
1-cochains a0,2 and a1,3 such that da0,2 = −a0,1a1,2 and da1,3 = −a1,2a2,3. The triple product
⟨u1, u2, u3⟩is then the subset of H2(A) consisting of the cohomology classes −[a0,1a1,3 + a0,2a2,3],
for all such choices of defining systems. Due to the ambiguity in the choices made, ⟨u1, u2, u3⟩may
be viewed as a coset of u1 · H1(A) + H1(A) · u3 in H2(A).
Example 2.2. Let (A, d) be the exterior algebra on generators a1, a2, b in degree 1 and differential
given by dai = 0 and db = a1a2. Letting ui = [ai] ∈H1(A), we have that the triple Massey
products ⟨u1, u1, u2⟩= {[a1b]} and ⟨u1, u2, u2⟩= {[ba2]} are defined, have 0 indeterminacy, and are
non-vanishing; in fact, the two cohomology classes generate H2(A). Therefore, A is not formal.
Massey products enjoy the following (partial) functoriality properties.

φ∗(⟨u1, . . . , un⟩) ⊆⟨φ∗(u1), . . . , φ∗(un)⟩.
Moreover, if φ is a quasi-isomorphism, then (2.4) holds as equality.
In particular, if ⟨u1, . . . , un⟩is defined, then ⟨φ∗(u1), . . . , φ∗(un)⟩is also defined; and if, in ad-
dition, ⟨φ∗(u1), . . . , φ∗(un)⟩is non-vanishing, then ⟨u1, . . . , un⟩is also non-vanishing. As another
consequence, the following holds: if A ≃B, then all Massey products in H∗(A) vanish if and only if
all Massey products in H∗(B) vanish.
Finally, if the map φ: A →B is a q-quasi-isomorphism, for some q ≥0, then (2.4) holds as
equality in degrees up to q + 1. Thus, if A ≃q B, then all Massey products in H≤q+1(A) vanish if and
only if all Massey products in H≤q+1(B) vanish.
The vanishing of Massey products provides a well known obstruction to formality. An analogous
statement holds for partial formality, see e.g. [105].
Proposition 2.4. Let (A, d) be a k-cdga. If A formal, then all Massey products in H∗(A) vanish.
Furthermore, if A is q-formal, for some q ≥1, then all Massey products in H≤q+1(A) vanish.
In general, formality is stronger than the mere vanishing of all Massey products; in fact, it is
equivalent to the uniform vanishing of all such products. In the coming sections we develop alter-
native obstructions to formality via resonance varieties of Koszul complexes and Hilbert series of
Koszul modules, which in many cases detect non-formality more effectively than individual Massey
products.
3. The Koszul construction
The purpose of this section is to introduce the Koszul construction associated to a connected
cdga (A, dA) with finite-dimensional first cohomology. This construction assigns to A two canoni-
cally dual complexes, one cohomological and one homological, defined over polynomial rings that
encode complementary aspects of H1(A). Their homology and cohomology give rise to Koszul
modules, which play a central role throughout the paper.


## Page 27


KOSZUL MODULES, HOLONOMY, AND RESONANCE
27
We emphasize three guiding themes. First, the Koszul complexes are naturally governed by
Eckmann–Hilton duality, relating cohomological and homological viewpoints through graded linear
duality. Second, the behavior of Koszul homology is sensitive to the full differential of A, not merely
to its cohomology algebra. Third, the Koszul modules admit natural filtrations whose associated
spectral sequences will be used later to compare A with H∗(A) and to relate algebraic invariants to
topological and geometric data.
3.1. The two coefficient rings: S and T. Throughout this section, A = (A•, dA) denotes a con-
nected cdga over a field k of characteristic 0, and we assume 0 < dimk H1(A) < ∞.
Since A is connected, the differential dA : A0 →A1 vanishes, and so we may identify H1(A) =
Z1(A) = ker(dA : A1 →A2). Set E = V(H1(A)) and write E1 = H1(A) and E1 = (E1)∨for the dual
vector space.
We define the cohomological coefficient ring as
S B Sym(E1) = Sym(H1(A)),
the symmetric algebra on E1 = H1(A), and the homological coefficient ring as
T B Sym(E1) = Sym(H1(A)),
the symmetric algebra on E1 = H1(A). The natural perfect pairing E1⊗kE1 →k induces a canonical
isomorphism S  T of (ungraded) k-algebras, but S and T represent two complementary perspec-
tives on this object, reflecting Eckmann–Hilton duality between cohomological and homological
viewpoints.
Fix a basis {e1, . . . , en} for E1 = H1(A), and let {x1, . . . , xn} be the dual basis for E1 = H1(A).
Then S = k[x1, . . . , xn] and T = k[e1, . . . , en] as polynomial rings, with the isomorphism S  T
given by x j 7→ej. Let m = (x1, . . . , xn) ⊂S and n = (e1, . . . , en) ⊂T be the respective augmentation
ideals.
Both S and T carry natural graded Hopf algebra structures, with primitive generators in degree
1: S via the generators x j and T via the generators e j. Under the isomorphism S  T, these
Hopf algebra structures are identified, but the cohomological and homological gradings on S and
T remain distinct, reflecting the dual roles they play in the Koszul construction developed in the
following subsections.

Remark 3.10. Proposition 3.8 shows that the Koszul homology and cohomology modules of A con-

Koszul spectral sequence determines the cohomological one, and vice versa. For this reason, and
because Bi(A) interacts more directly with Alexander-type invariants and filtrations, we shall focus
in the remainder of this paper primarily on Koszul homology.
3.6. Filtrations on Koszul modules. We now introduce the natural filtrations on the Koszul (co)ho-
mology modules and address the fundamental question of their separation, which will play a recur-
ring role throughout the paper.
The cohomological Koszul T-modules Bi(A) = Hi(K•(A)) carry a natural descending m-adic
filtration, inherited from the m-adic filtration on K•(A):
Bi(A) ⊇mBi(A) ⊇m2Bi(A) ⊇· · ·

Proposition 3.11. The m-adic filtration on each cohomological Koszul module Bi(A) is separated.
In particular, the m-adic filtration is Hausdorff and the associated graded grm Bi(A) determines
Bi(A) up to filtration.
Proof. Since K•(A) is a bounded complex of finitely generated free S -modules, each Bi(A) is a
finitely generated S -module. The Krull intersection theorem then gives T
k≥0 mkBi(A) = 0.
□
By contrast, the m-adic filtration on the homological Koszul modules Bi(A) need not be sep-
arated.
The basic example is A = CE(sol2), discussed in Section 3.7 below: there B1(A) 
k[x]/(x −1), and since x −1 is a unit in k[x]/(x −1), one has mB1(A) = B1(A) and hence

This asymmetry reflects the S-vs-T duality of Section 3.1: the ring S acts on K•(A) via multipli-
cation, making the filtration compatible with the algebra structure and ensuring separation via the
Krull intersection theorem; while T acts on K•(A) via the comodule structure, which need not be
compatible with separation.
Separation of Bi(A) is guaranteed in two important cases: when A has positive weights (Propo-
sition 7.4), and when Bi(A) is a finitely generated torsion S -module. It plays a role in spectral
sequence convergence (Theorem 5.2(4)), in isomorphism theorems under quasi-isomorphisms (The-
orem 8.6(1)), and in the comparison with Alexander invariants developed in Section 15.

3.8. Algebraic structures on Koszul modules. The Koszul cochain complex K•(A) is a dg-algebra
over S (Proposition 3.1), and the Koszul chain complex K•(A) is its dual dg-coalgebra over T. These
structures pass to (co)homology, equipping the Koszul modules with additional algebraic structure
beyond their bare S -module and T-comodule structures.
Proposition 3.13. Let (A, dA) be a connected, finite-type k-cdga.
(1) The cohomological Koszul modules B•(A) = H•(K•(A)) form a graded-commutative S -
algebra, with product induced by the dg-algebra structure on K•(A).
(2) The homological Koszul modules B•(A) = H•(K•(A)) form a graded-cocommutative T-
coalgebra, with coproduct induced by the dg-coalgebra structure on K•(A).
Proof. Both statements follow immediately from the fact that the product on K•(A) commutes with
the differential δA, and its dual coproduct on K•(A) commutes with ∂A. The graded-commutativity of
the induced product on B•(A) follows from the graded-commutativity of K•(A) as a dg-algebra.
□
In addition to the S -algebra structure on B•(A) and the T-coalgebra structure on B•(A), the
cohomology algebra H∗(A) acts on both Koszul modules via cap and cup products.
Proposition 3.14. Let (A, dA) be a connected, finite-type k-cdga. The cohomology algebra H∗(A)
acts on both Koszul modules:
(1) Cap product action on B•(A): there is a left action

with the S -algebra structure on B•(A) and dualizes to the cap product action of (1) under

degree −p, commuting with ∂A by the Leibniz rule and the cocycle condition. Passing to homology
gives the cap product.
For (2): multiplication by α ∈Zp(A) in the A-factor defines a cochain map α ∧(−): K•(A) →
K•+p(A) of degree +p, commuting with δA by the dg-algebra structure on K•(A). Passing to coho-
mology gives the cup product. The duality between the two actions follows from the graded duality
between K•(A) and K•(A).
□
Remark 3.15. When b1(A) = 1, the cohomological Koszul modules Bi(A) are cyclic k[x]-modules,
and in all computed examples the nonzero modules Bi(A) and B j(A) for i , j have disjoint support
in mSpec(k[x]) = A1, forcing the cup product to vanish. Whether the multiplicative structure on
B•(A) or the comultiplicative structure on B•(A) carries information beyond the S -module or T-
comodule structures remains open.
The cohomology algebra H∗(A) acts on both Koszul modules via cap and cup products. This
action is used in the present paper primarily to identify the connecting morphism δe in the Koszul–
Gysin sequence of a Hirsch extension as cap product with [e] ∈H2(B) (Theorem 10.6 and Corol-
lary 10.7). More generally, if A carries a positive-weight decomposition (Section 7.2), then the
H∗(A)-action preserves the total-weight filtration on B•(A) and induces the corresponding action
on each associated graded piece. By Theorem 7.6 and Corollary 7.7, the associated graded Koszul
homology is canonically identified with B•(H∗(A)), so the H∗(A)-action interacts with weights in
a completely controlled way. In particular, for weight-preserving morphisms (Section 8.7), this
compatibility implies multiplicative Bq-formality for all q.
Whether the H∗(A)-action carries deeper geometric or topological information beyond these for-
mal properties—such as governing formality obstructions, controlling the decomposition of B•(A)
along resonance components, or interacting with other filtrations—remains an interesting open
question.

4. Koszul complexes and Poincar´e duality
In this section, we investigate how Poincar´e duality interacts with the Koszul constructions in-
troduced in Section 3. Our goal is to understand how additional duality assumptions on a cdga
translate into structural constraints on its Koszul complexes and their (co)homology, beyond those
arising from linear duality alone.
Two distinct sources of duality enter the picture. On the one hand, the homological and coho-
mological Koszul complexes are related by Eckmann–Hilton duality. On the other hand, a Poincar´e
duality structure on a cdga—or on its cohomology algebra—provides canonical identifications be-
tween graded pieces in complementary degrees. When these two forms of duality coexist, they
interact in a subtle but highly structured way, producing adjunctions between Koszul differentials
and inducing self-dual or palindromic behavior in Koszul (co)homology.
After recalling the notion of a Poincar´e duality cdga, we analyze these interactions at the level
of Aomoto and Koszul complexes. A key point is that Poincar´e duality gives rise to PD-adjoint
differentials, which should not be confused with naive matrix transposes. This distinction becomes
essential when passing from cochain to chain complexes and explains phenomena invisible at the
level of cohomology alone.
4.1. Poincar´e duality cdgas. Throughout this subsection, A = (A•, d) denotes a connected cdga
over a field k of characteristic 0. We assume A is of finite type when needed.

gebraic invariants insensitive to individual elements of A1—such as Koszul modules and resonance
varieties—this involution disappears, and Poincar´e duality reduces to a palindromic symmetry in
the formal dimension m.

Geometrically, the PD2n+1-algebra structure of H∗(A(n)) reflects the fact that A(n) models the
cohomology of the compact Heisenberg nilmanifold of dimension 2n + 1.
Remark 4.9. The Heisenberg cdgas A(n) = CE(h(n)) exhibit subtle formality properties. It is shown
by M˘acinic [64] that A(n) is (n −1)-formal but not n-formal, while A(1) = CE(h(1)) is not 1-formal.
Thus, among the Heisenberg Lie algebras, h(1) is the only case in which non-formality already
appears at the first stage. We will come back to this subject in Section 18.10.

5. Spectral sequences for Koszul (co)homology
In this section, we study the Koszul complexes of a cdga through the lens of spectral sequences.
Filtering the Koszul (co)chain complexes by symmetric degree produces two dual spectral se-
quences, one cohomological and one homological, which interpolate between the Koszul complexes
of A and those of its cohomology algebra H∗(A).
The E1-pages recover the classical Koszul complexes of H∗(A) and H•(A), while the higher dif-
ferentials encode successive Massey products along the universal Aomoto class. In particular, these
spectral sequences make precise how non-formality of A manifests as higher-order cancellations in
Koszul (co)homology.
The main results of this section establish Theorem A from the Introduction in multiplicative and
comultiplicative refinements.
When A satisfies Poincar´e duality, the two spectral sequences are related by the adjunctions
described in Section 4, and their behavior reflects the same palindromic and self-dual phenomena.
Several examples—most notably the Chevalley–Eilenberg algebra of the Heisenberg Lie algebra—
illustrate how the two spectral sequences encode the same Koszul invariants in dual but asymmetric
ways.
5.1. The cohomological Koszul spectral sequence. Let (A, dA) be a connected k-cdga over a field
k of characteristic 0, and assume H∗(A) is finite-dimensional in each degree. Let S = Sym(H1(A))
and let m ⊂S be the augmentation ideal. We filter the Koszul cochain complex K•(A) = (A•⊗kS, δA)
by the descending m-adic filtration

Theorem 5.1 provides the multiplicative refinement of Theorem A announced in the Introduction.
The corresponding degeneration result under formality or q-formality, for both the cohomological
and homological spectral sequences simultaneously, is recorded in Corollary 5.4 below.

Remark 5.3. The homological and cohomological Koszul spectral sequences exhibit a fundamental
asymmetry with respect to strong convergence. The cohomological case is handled in the proof
above via the Krull intersection theorem. For the homological spectral sequence, strong convergence
may fail: the n-adic filtration on Bi(A) is separated if and only if SuppT(Bi(A)) ⊆V(n), i.e., Bi(A)
is supported at the origin of mSpec T.
When dA , 0, the support of Bi(A) may contain points away from the origin, and such com-
ponents are entirely invisible to the spectral sequence: if n ⊈√AnnT M, then n · M = M, so
T
k≥0 nkM = M , 0 and grn M = 0. The module M contributes nothing to E∞, even if the spectral
sequence collapses at E2.
This phenomenon is illustrated in detail by A = CE(sol2) in Example 5.5 below: the module
B1(A)  T/(x −1) is supported at {x = 1}, away from the origin, and is completely invisible to the
homological spectral sequence despite the latter collapsing at E2.

More generally, the higher differentials dr and dr (r ≥2) in the two spectral sequences record
successive Massey product obstructions to formality: the later the sequences collapse, the more
severely the full nonlinear structure of A departs from its linearization H∗(A). In this sense, the
pages of both spectral sequences provide a filtration of the gap between A and its cohomology
algebra, with the E2-page capturing the purely linear (formal) approximation.

associated graded of the Koszul modules, not of the modules themselves.
6. Associated graded Koszul modules and Hilbert series
In this section we introduce the associated graded versions of Koszul modules and analyze their
basic properties. Although Koszul modules arise naturally as filtered objects, their associated graded
modules capture much of the relevant algebraic information in a more tractable form. The key
linearization result—Proposition 6.1 below—identifies the associated graded of the Koszul complex
of A with the Koszul complex of its cohomology algebra H∗(A). This identification underlies the
tangent cone theorem for resonance varieties (Theorem 9.13) and the Hilbert series comparisons
developed later in the section. In particular, the passage to the associated graded setting allows us to
encode the size and growth of Koszul modules by Hilbert series, which will later be identified with
topological invariants such as Chen ranks and Alexander-type data.

Remark 6.7. The restriction to i = 1 in Corollary 6.6 is essential. For i ≥2, the group grk Bi(A)
is dual to TorE
k−i(A, k)k, which lies on the strand of slope k/(k −i) > 1 in the Betti table of A over
E . Propagation of vanishing along non-linear strands is not controlled by minimality alone, and the
analogous statement for i ≥2 may fail. The case i = 1 is special precisely because it corresponds
to the linear strand: once a term in the linear strand vanishes, all subsequent terms must vanish
too, since a nonzero differential into a zero module is impossible, and minimality then forces the
source to be zero. This elementary principle—that the linear strand of a minimal free resolution
cannot revive once it terminates—is well known in commutative algebra; see [42] for the BGG
perspective.
Remark 6.8. Theorem 6.4 has a perhaps unexpected consequence. Even when A has infinite-
dimensional cohomology in degrees > 1, so that the Koszul modules Bi(A) need not be finitely gen-
erated over S = Sym(H1(A)), their associated graded modules exhibit a strong degreewise finiteness
property.
More precisely, although gr Bi(A) may be neither finitely generated over S nor finite-dimensional
as a whole, Theorem 6.4 implies that it is locally finite with respect to the internal grading inherited
from A: for each internal degree d and each filtration degree k, the vector space (grk Bi(A))d is finite-
dimensional and determined functorially by the E = V A1-module structure of A, via the groups
TorE (A, k).
Thus, the failure of finite generation of Bi(A) over S is a global phenomenon, reflecting the
accumulation of infinitely many finite-dimensional pieces, rather than a breakdown of finiteness in
any fixed internal degree.

Part II. Structural properties
In this part, we study additional structures and functoriality properties of Koszul modules that
sharpen their algebraic and geometric behavior. We first introduce positive weight decompositions
on cdgas, which, when present, promote Koszul modules from filtered to genuinely graded objects
and impose strong linearity constraints on resonance and support loci. We then analyze the natu-
rality of Koszul modules with respect to cdga morphisms, clarifying the role of base change and
identifying precise obstructions to functoriality. Together, these results provide the technical foun-
dation needed for the comparisons with topological and group-theoretic invariants developed in the
later parts of the paper.
Throughout this part, we work with the identification S  T of Section 3.1 and regard all Koszul
modules as filtered S -modules; the distinction between S and T plays no further role.
7. Positive weights and the weight spectral sequence
Positive weights provide a natural framework in which the Koszul modules of a genuine cdga
acquire honest gradings, rather than merely filtrations. In this setting, the algebraic structures intro-
duced in Part I become more rigid and more transparent.
When a cdga (A, d) admits positive weights, the resulting multigraded structure imposes strong
linearity constraints on the support loci Ri,s(A) and Ri,s(A). In favorable cases, these jump loci
decompose as finite unions of coordinate linear subspaces of H1(A), a phenomenon that plays a
central role in the applications to resonance and holonomy developed later in the paper.
7.1. Positive weights on a cdga. We begin by recalling the notion of positive weights for com-
mutative differential graded algebras, which formalizes the idea that the algebra admits a grading
compatible with both multiplication and differential, and in which all generators carry strictly posi-
tive weight.
Definition 7.1. A connected cdga (A, dA) over a field k of characteristic 0 is said to have positive
weights if there exist decompositions
Ai =
L
α>0 Ai,α
for all i > 0 such that
Ai,α · A j,β ⊆Ai+ j,α+β
and
dA(Ai,α) ⊆Ai+1,α
for all integers i, j > 0 and α, β > 0. (By convention, A0 = k has weight 0.) In particular, every
cdga (A, 0) with zero differential has positive weights: assign weight i to Ai for i > 0 and weight 0
to A0 = k.
The notion of positive weights appears in the literature in several equivalent formulations [14,
113, 110], all valid over any field k with char k = 0. In the minimal Sullivan model (ΛV, d),
one assigns a positive integer weight to each generator and extends multiplicatively, requiring d to
preserve weight; see [14, Def. 2.1]. Equivalently, by [14, Props. 2.3 and 2.7], (A, d) has positive
weights if and only if there exists a degree-0 derivation Ψ: A →A commuting with d whose
eigenvalues on A>0 are strictly positive integers, with the weight decomposition Ai,α = ker(Ψ −α ·

id)|Ai recovering Definition 7.1. By [14, Thm. 2.7], having positive weights is independent of the
ground field: Q-positive weights, C-positive weights, and k-positive weights are all equivalent.
Remark 7.2. For a minimal Sullivan cdga generated in degree 1, positive weights in the sense of
Definition 7.1 are equivalent to the existence of positive Hirsch weights [110]: one assigns weight k
to each generator adjoined at the k-th stage of the Hirsch extension tower, extends multiplicatively,
and requires the differential to preserve total weight. This formulation is the one most directly
relevant to the present work: the CE models of nilpotent Lie algebras and the cdga models of
Sasakian manifolds both arise naturally as Hirsch extensions, and the weight grading on their Koszul
complexes is induced by the Hirsch weights on the generators.

Remark 7.9. The filtration considered in Theorem 7.5 is formally analogous to the weight filtration
arising from a positively weighted Sullivan minimal model in rational homotopy theory. In that
setting, the associated spectral sequence computes the graded Lie algebra of rational homotopy
groups from the cohomology algebra. The present construction is the purely algebraic counterpart:
the E1-page depends only on H∗(A), while higher differentials measure the deviation of A from
formality.
Remark 7.10. It is natural to ask whether the existence of positive weights alone imposes linearity
constraints on resonance varieties. More precisely, if a finite-type cdga (A, dA) carries a system of
positive weights (without necessarily arising from a geometric model), must each resonance variety
Ri(A) be a finite union of linear subspaces? To the best of our knowledge, this question remains
open (see [101]).

8. Naturality properties of Koszul modules
The Koszul modules associated to a cdga are functorial only in a restricted sense. Since their
definition depends on the symmetric algebra S = Sym(H1(A)), a morphism of cdgas typically
induces a comparison map only after a suitable change of scalars. In this section, we make precise
the naturality properties of Koszul modules under cdga morphisms, identify situations in which
stronger functoriality holds, and explain how failures of naturality reflect obstructions to formality,
both for algebras and for maps.
8.1. Naturality of Koszul modules. We begin by describing the most general functoriality enjoyed
by Koszul modules. A morphism of cdgas induces a natural comparison map between the corre-
sponding Koszul complexes only after restriction of scalars along the induced map on symmetric
algebras. Although this construction is formally functorial, it is often too weak for applications,
motivating the stronger notions considered in subsequent subsections.

where H1(A) = (H1(A))∨.

Corollary 8.2. The map K•(φ) is an isomorphism of filtered chain complexes, and hence induces
isomorphisms Bi(A)  Bi( ¯A) for all i, in either of the following cases:
(1) φ is a cdga isomorphism;

Proof. In case (1), φ is invertible at every degree, so φ ⊗Φ is an isomorphism of chain complexes.
In case (2), since A and ¯A are generated in degree 1, the degree-2 piece of φ is the map induced
by φ1 on A1 ∧A1; since φ1 is an isomorphism, so is φ2 : A2 →¯A2. Together with φ1 being an
isomorphism, this ensures that φ ⊗Φ is an isomorphism at the chain level in degrees 0, 1, and 2,
hence an isomorphism of filtered chain complexes.
□
Example 8.3. Let A = V⟨a⟩with da = 0, and let ¯A = CE(sol2) = V⟨a, b⟩with db = −a ∧b, the

that Koszul homology is sensitive to the cochain-level data of φ, not merely to the induced map on
cohomology.

8.3. Behavior under quasi-isomorphisms. We now examine how Koszul modules behave under
quasi-isomorphisms. Although Koszul homology is not invariant under arbitrary quasi-isomor-
phisms, the presence of an isomorphism on H1 allows one to compare the associated Koszul com-
plexes over a common polynomial ring. In this situation, the m-adic filtration gives rise to a natural
spectral sequence that controls the effect of a q-(quasi-)isomorphism on Koszul homology and on
its associated graded modules.

Corollary 8.8. Let φ: A →¯A be a q-quasi-isomorphism between connected, q-finite k-cdgas.
(1) If A and ¯A both admit positive weight decompositions, then Ki(φ): Bi(A) −→Bi( ¯A) is an
S -linear isomorphism for all i ≤q.
(2) Ki(φ): Bi(A) −→Bi( ¯A) is an S -linear isomorphism for all i ≤q, with no additional hy-

Proof. (1) By Proposition 7.4, positive weights imply that the m-adic filtrations on Bi(A) and Bi( ¯A)

weight hypothesis is unnecessary. The conclusion follows from Theorem 8.7(1).

8.4. Naturality of multiplicative structures. The S -module and comodule naturality established
in Sections 8.2 and 8.3 does not capture the full algebraic structure carried by the Koszul mod-
ules. In this subsection we show that the graded-commutative algebra structure on B•(A), the
graded-cocommutative coalgebra structure on B•(A), and the H∗(A)-module structures of Propo-
sition 3.14 are all natural with respect to cdga morphisms inducing isomorphisms on H1. Under q-
quasi-isomorphisms, these multiplicative structures are preserved at the level of associated graded
modules, and under the appropriate separation hypotheses they are preserved at the module level
itself.

that is naturally measured by Koszul homology.

When A and ¯A are both fully formal, Bq-formality for all q reduces to the requirement that Bi(φ)
and Bi(φ∗) are related by S -module isomorphisms on both sides for every i. By definition, q-
formality implies Bq-formality, but the converse need not hold in general: Bq-formality is the
Koszul-homological shadow of q-formality, and may be strictly weaker.
Theorem 8.11. Let φ: (A, dA) →( ¯A, d ¯A) be a cdga morphism between connected 1-finite cdgas that
are both q-formal, and that induces an isomorphism on H1. If φ is q-formal, then φ is Bq-formal.
Consequently, if φ is not Bq-formal for some q, then φ is not q-formal.
Proof. Let bφ = K•(φ): K•(A) →K•( ¯A) be the induced filtered chain morphism of Proposition 8.1.
By definition, φ is q-formal if it fits into a commutative diagram of the form (2.2), where each hori-
zontal map is a q-quasi-isomorphism. By Proposition 8.1, each step in the zigzag yields a covariant
chain map between the corresponding Koszul complexes over a common symmetric algebra.
Since A and ¯A are both q-formal, the respective formality zigzags, together with Theorem 8.6(1),
provide S -module isomorphisms Bi(A)  Bi(H∗(A)) and Bi( ¯A)  Bi(H∗( ¯A)) for all i ≤q. By
Theorem 8.6(2), each step in the zigzag for φ induces an isomorphism on grm Bj for j ≤q. Since
the diagram (2.2) commutes up to homotopy and naturality of the Koszul construction is preserved
at each step, composing the isomorphisms across the zigzag yields the commutative square (8.7) for
each i ≤q, with vertical isomorphisms induced by the respective formality zigzags. The contrapos-
itive gives the obstruction statement.
□
Corollary 8.12. Let φ: A →¯A be a cdga morphism between formal cdgas, inducing an isomor-
phism on H∗. If φ fails to be Bq-formal for some q, then φ is not formal.
Theorem 8.13. Let g be a finite-dimensional 2-step nilpotent Lie algebra over k, set A = CE(g),
and let α ∈Aut(g) with φ = CE(α) ∈Aut(A).
(1) φ is filtered-formal.
