67
(2) If A is 1-formal, then φ is B1-formal.
Proof. For (1): since g is 2-step nilpotent, we have gr g = g as graded Lie algebras, with g1 = g/Z(g)
and g2 = Z(g), so A = CE(g) is already filtered-formal. Any α ∈Aut(g) preserves the lower central
series filtration, hence φ = CE(α) is compatible with the filtered structure and induces CE(gr α) = φ
on the associated graded. Therefore φ is filtered-formal.
For (2): since A is 1-formal, the 1-formality zigzag provides S -module isomorphisms B1(A) 
B1(H∗(A)). For any α ∈Aut(g), the action of α on Z(g) is determined by its action on g/Z(g) via the
bracket map V2(g/Z(g)) →Z(g). Since this bracket map is exactly the cup product on H∗(A) (up to
duality), the action of φ∗= H∗(φ) on H∗(A) sees the same data, and hence the square (8.7) commutes
for i = 1 under the isomorphisms provided by the 1-formality zigzag. Thus φ is B1-formal.
□
Part (2) shows that the obstruction of Theorem 8.11 cannot detect non-1-formality of CE au-
tomorphisms in the 2-step nilpotent setting: every such automorphism is automatically B1-formal
when A is 1-formal. This raises the question of whether Bq-formality is strictly weaker than q-
formality for morphisms of CE type.
Question 8.14. Let g be a finite-dimensional 2-step nilpotent Lie algebra with A = CE(g) being 1-
formal, and let α ∈Aut(g). Is φ = CE(α) necessarily 1-formal? More generally, does Bq-formality
imply q-formality for morphisms of CE type, or does a gap between the two notions appear already
for 3-step nilpotent Lie algebras?
The following example shows that B1-formality is a non-trivial condition on morphisms: there
exist CE automorphisms of 3-step nilpotent algebras that fail to be B1-formal, in contrast to the
automatic B1-formality established in Theorem 8.13(2) for the 2-step nilpotent case.

Problem 8.18. Does there exist a cdga morphism φ: A →¯A between q-formal cdgas that is Bq-
formal but not multiplicatively Bq-formal? By Remark 8.17, conditions (1) and (2) are equivalent
for PD-cdgas, so any such morphism must arise outside the Poincar´e duality setting. In particular,
does the IA-automorphism of Example 8.15 yield such a counterexample for q = 1?

imply multiplicative Bq-formality? Conversely, under what additional hypotheses, if any, does
multiplicative Bq-formality imply that φ is q-formal?
8.7. Weight-preserving morphisms and Bq-formality. We now identify a natural class of mor-
phisms for which multiplicative Bq-formality holds automatically for all q: those that preserve the
positive-weight decompositions of Section 7.1. This provides a large supply of examples and clari-
fies when the open problems of Section 8.6 are genuinely nontrivial.
Definition 8.20. Let (A, dA) and ( ¯A, d ¯A) be two connected cdgas with positive weights. A cdga
morphism φ: A →¯A is weight-preserving if φ(Ai,α) ⊆¯Ai,α for all i ≥1 and α > 0.

Theorem 8.21. Let φ: A →¯A be a weight-preserving q-quasi-isomorphism between connected,
finite-type cdgas with positive weights. Then φ is multiplicatively Bq-formal.
Proof. Since φ is weight-preserving, the chain map φ ⊗idS : K•(A) →K•( ¯A) is compatible with
the total-weight filtrations on both sides. By Proposition 7.4, both weight filtrations are separated,
so Corollary 8.8 gives S -module isomorphisms Bi(A)  Bi(H∗(A)) and Bi( ¯A)  Bi(H∗( ¯A)) for all
i. The induced map on associated graded complexes is K•(H∗(φ)), so the Bq-formality square (8.7)
commutes for all q.
For multiplicative Bq-formality: since φ is weight-preserving, φ ⊗idS is a morphism of weight-
graded dg-algebras, and the cup and cap product actions are weight-homogeneous by Proposi-
tion 3.14. The equivariance diagrams of Theorem 8.9(3) therefore hold at the weight-graded level,
giving multiplicative Bq-formality for all q.
□
Corollary 8.22. For each q ≥1, any morphism in the following classes of cdgas that is a q-quasi-
isomorphism is multiplicatively Bq-formal.
(1) Chevalley–Eilenberg maps CE(f): CE(¯g) →CE(g) induced by Lie algebra morphisms
f : g →¯g between finite-dimensional nilpotent Lie algebras, equipped with their lower
central series weight gradings (see Section 11.3).
(2) Pullback maps f ∗: A(Y) →A(X) between Morgan–Gysin models of smooth quasi-projective
varieties, induced by morphisms f : X →Y, with weights wt(Ap,q) = p + 2q (see Sec-
tion 21.1).
Problem 8.23. Does the failure of multiplicative B1-formality for a CE automorphism φ = CE(α)—
which by Theorem 8.21 cannot occur when φ is a weight-preserving quasi-isomorphism—have de-
tectable consequences for the Hilbert series or support loci of B1(A), beyond those already detected
by the failure of B1-formality at the level of graded S -modules?
Part III. Resonance and holonomy
9. Resonance varieties, tangent cones, and positive weights
In this section we introduce resonance varieties associated to a cdga and develop their basic
properties from the perspective of Koszul (co)homology. Resonance varieties arise as algebraic
jump loci controlling the behavior of Koszul complexes under specialization, and provide a precise
algebraic counterpart to the characteristic varieties of spaces and groups. They play a central role
in relating homological invariants, support loci, and formality properties, and will later serve as the
main algebraic input for the topological applications developed in Parts III and IV.
The main structural results of this section are the tangent cone theorems stated as Theorem F
in the Introduction: the inclusion TC0(Rq,s(A)) ⊆Rq,s(H∗(A)), valid for all q-finite cdgas (The-
orem 9.13), and its upgrade to equality under q-formality (Theorem 9.17). Together these results
show that cohomology governs the first-order infinitesimal structure of resonance, while formality
forces exact agreement.
9.1. Resonance varieties and support loci. We begin by defining resonance varieties in terms of
the Koszul complexes associated to a cdga. From this viewpoint, resonance arises as a homological
jump phenomenon: it records those linear forms for which the specialized Koszul differential fails

below.

preserving characteristic varieties and Alexander invariants in low degrees; the role of the group

related. Under finite-type assumptions, they agree after taking unions over degrees, and differ in a

The determinantal scheme structure on Ri,s(A) arises naturally from the linear algebra of the
cochain complex and is well suited for detecting cohomology jump loci. The annihilator scheme
structure on Ri,s(A) encodes the support of the Koszul module Bi(A) and is the one relevant for
questions concerning decomposition, separability, and asymptotic behavior of homological invari-
ants. In particular, for problems related to Chen ranks and their infinitesimal counterparts, it is the
annihilator-defined scheme on R1(A) that governs whether additive formulas hold in high degrees:
under suitable hypotheses (see Sections 19.4 and 19.5), reducedness of this scheme governs the
extent to which B1(A) surjects onto a direct sum of modules supported on the irreducible compo-
nents of resonance, with the length of the kernel controlling the effective range of the Chen ranks
formula [4].
Since the results of this paper concern primarily the underlying varieties and their geometric
properties—such as linearity, conicality, and disjointness of components—we will not pursue these
scheme-theoretic distinctions further here.

Remark 9.15. The tangent cone inclusion for the resonance varieties Ri(A) was first proved by
Budur and Rubi´o [16], thereby resolving an open problem of Suciu [101, Prob. 3.2]. Their proof
uses L∞-pairs and derived deformation theory, identifying the analytic germ of the resonance variety
at the origin with a formal deformation functor, and recovering the tangent cone by linearization of
the universal L∞differential. A different approach was also explored in [32]. The proof given
here is entirely algebraic, relying only on the linearization of the Koszul complex (Proposition 6.1)
and elementary commutative algebra. In addition, the argument applies verbatim to the resonance
support loci Ri,s(A), yielding tangent cone inclusions that do not seem to have appeared previously
in the literature. Moreover, the argument applies under the minimal finiteness hypothesis that A is

9.4. Collapse, vanishing, and finite-length Koszul modules. Theorem 9.13 gives a one-sided
inclusion TC0(Ri,s(A)) ⊆Ri,s(H∗(A)) valid for all finite-type cdgas, and in general this inclusion
may be strict: the tangent cone of Ri(A) at the origin may be a proper subset of Ri(H∗(A)). When
the cohomological Koszul spectral sequence collapses early, however, the inclusion becomes an
equality and one can recover the tangent cone exactly when the resonance of H∗(A) vanishes; early
collapse also forces finite-length behavior on the Koszul module.

9.5. Positive weights and resonance. We now combine the tangent cone theorem with the coni-
cality of resonance varieties arising from positive weights.
Theorem 9.21. Let (A, d) be a connected, finite-type cdga over a field of characteristic 0, admitting
positive weights. Then, for all i, s,
(1) The resonance varieties Ri,s(A) and support loci Ri,s(A) are conical.
(2) Ri,s(A) ⊆Ri,s(H∗(A)) and Ri,s(A) ⊆Ri,s(H∗(A)).
Proof. Claim (1) is immediate from Corollary 7.12.
By Theorem 9.13, TC0(Ri,s(A)) ⊆Ri,s(H∗(A)) and TC0(Ri,s(A)) ⊆Ri,s(H∗(A)). Since Ri,s(A)
is conical, it coincides with its tangent cone at 0, i.e., Ri,s(A) = TC0(Ri,s(A)), and similarly for
Ri,s(A). Claim (2) follows.
□
Thus, failure of either conicality or the resonance inclusion provides an obstruction to the exis-
tence of positive weights on a cdga; see Example 9.26 for a concrete way in which this criterion
applies.
Previous results on linearity of resonance varieties under positive weights were obtained in a
topological setting, assuming the existence of a finite-type CW-complex realizing the model and a
comparison map preserving rational structures [35, 65]. Theorem 9.21 shows that conicality of res-
onance is an intrinsic property of cdgas admitting positive weights, independent of any topological
realization of the cdga.

all i, s, the difference
Ri,s(A) \ Ri,s(A)
is a conical subset of H1(A). Moreover, if a ∈H1(A) lies outside Ri−1,1(A), then the spectral

P Ri,s(A) = P Ri,s(A),
as projective subvarieties of P(H1(A)).
Proof. All constructions (Koszul complexes, Koszul modules, and change-of-rings spectral se-
quence) are compatible with the positive grading on A, hence inherit a natural k∗-action induced
by scaling on H1(A). Since Ri,s(A) and Ri,s(A) are k∗-invariant by Theorem 9.21, their difference
is a conical subset as well.
For the second statement, note that the condition Bi−1(A) ⊗S κa = 0 is preserved under scaling
a 7→λa, which corresponds to the induced k∗-action on S , under which ma is sent to mλa, and
Bi−1(A) is equivariant as a graded S -module. Hence the degeneration criterion in Proposition 9.9 is
invariant along k∗-orbits, and the identification of jump and support loci holds projectively.
□
In the presence of positive weights, resonance and support loci are naturally k∗-invariant and
therefore descend to well-defined projective subvarieties of P(H1(A)). From this perspective, the
comparison between jump and support loci is fundamentally a statement about projective cones in
P(H1(A)), governed by the degeneration behavior of the Koszul spectral sequence along k∗-orbits.
In particular, nontrivial resonance corresponds to the existence of nonempty projective subvarieties,
while vanishing resonance amounts to the collapse of these projective cones to the empty projective
locus. In the next subsection we show that, under suitable q-formality hypotheses, this collapse
occurs on a Zariski open subset of the space of differentials
9.6. Vanishing resonance for cdgas. Particularly rigid—and algebraically significant—is the situ-
ation where resonance varieties vanish. In this subsection, we show that vanishing resonance is not
an exceptional phenomenon in the category of cdga’s, but rather occurs generically once the under-
lying graded-commutative algebra is fixed and suitable partial formality assumptions are imposed.
Let A• be a finite-type, connected graded-commutative k-algebra. We consider cdga structures
(A, d) whose differential is compatible with the grading and trivial up to a prescribed degree.
Definition 9.23. Fix an integer q ≥1. Let Dq(A) denote the set of differentials d: A• →A•+1 such
that (A, d) is a cdga which is q-equivalent to (A, 0), that is, (A, d) is q-formal.
Note that Dq(A) is naturally a Zariski-constructible subset of the affine variety of degree-1 differ-
entials on A. Indeed, q-formality is detected by finitely many cohomological conditions in degrees
≤q + 1, which can be expressed as rank conditions on linear maps depending polynomially on the
coefficients of the differential.
Theorem 9.24. Let A• be a finite-type, connected graded-commutative algebra admitting a positive-
weight decomposition. Fix q ≥1. Then there exists a Zariski open subset
Uq ⊆Dq(A)
such that, for every d ∈Uq,
Ri(A, d) ⊆{0},
for all i ≤q.


## Page 80


80
ALEXANDER I. SUCIU
Proof. Since A• admits a positive-weight decomposition, the resonance varieties Ri(A, d) are coni-
cal for every d ∈Dq(A) by Theorem 9.21(1).
By Corollary 5.4(2), q-formality of (A, d) implies that the cohomological Koszul spectral se-
quence collapses at the E2-page in total degrees ≤q.
For d in the Zariski open subset of Dq(A) on which the induced differential on grF Ai vanishes
for all i ≤q, the cohomology algebra H∗(A, d) coincides with H∗(A, 0) = A• in degrees ≤q, so
Ri(H∗(A, d)) = {0} for i ≤q. Proposition 9.20 then gives TC0(Ri(A, d)) = {0} for all i ≤q. Since
Ri(A, d) is conical, it equals its tangent cone at 0, so Ri(A, d) ⊆{0}, as claimed.
□
Remark 9.25. Theorem 9.24 shows that vanishing resonance is a generic phenomenon among
cdga structures carried by a fixed graded-commutative algebra, once partial formality and positive
weights are imposed. In this sense, nontrivial resonance reflects special features of the differential,
rather than of the underlying graded algebra.
Generic vanishing results of this type were first obtained by Papadima and Suciu [85, 87] in a
purely algebraic framework, by fixing a graded vector space A = k ⊕A1 ⊕A2 and studying the
resonance variety R1(A) as the graded-commutative multiplication on A varies. The theorem above
extends this philosophy to the setting of cdga’s and higher degrees, showing that the Koszul spectral
sequence provides a natural mechanism for detecting when resonance must vanish generically.
9.7. Examples and discussion. We conclude this section with some examples and further discus-
sion of the tangent cone theorem for cdgas.

under quasi-isomorphisms, their tangent cones at the origin are controlled solely by the cohomology
algebra, as guaranteed by Theorem 9.13.

Theorem 9.17 cannot be dropped: equality of tangent cones and cohomology-level resonance may
fail for non-formal cdgas, even when the model admits positive weights.
10. Resonance under algebraic constructions
Many cdga models arising in geometry and topology are obtained from simpler ones by means
of tensor products, coproducts, and Hirsch extensions. In the next three subsections, we record sev-
eral results describing how resonance varieties behave under these constructions. These structural
results will be used repeatedly in later sections, and they also provide an alternative approach to the
vanishing phenomena for resonance observed in nilpotent Chevalley–Eilenberg models.
10.1. Resonance of tensor products and coproducts. The K¨unneth decompositions of Corol-

results of this type, obtained via Aomoto complex methods and valid only at depth s = 1 or in
degree q = 1, appear in [83, 108, 102, 86, 106].

mutativity. At the level of Koszul (co)homology, these symmetries manifest as dualities between
homological and cohomological resonance, which we record next.

while the support loci satisfy Ri,s(A) = Rm−i,s(A) directly.

Remark 10.9. Poincar´e duality symmetry does not, in general, imply propagation of resonance.
Indeed, if A is a PDm-cdga, then

upward propagation. In particular, a PDm-cdga may have large resonance in middle degrees while
Rm(A) = {0}, preventing propagation. This phenomenon was observed for closed orientable 3-
manifolds in [33]: if b1(M) is even and nonzero, then R1(M, k) = H1(M, k) while R3(M, k) = {0}
by Poincar´e duality, so resonance cannot propagate; see [33, Props. 4.3–4.4 and Cor. 4.5].

11. Chevalley–Eilenberg complexes
In this section we examine Chevalley–Eilenberg complexes as a bridge between Lie-theoretic
and topological invariants. Our focus is on how filtrations, gradings, and nilpotency properties of a
Lie algebra g are reflected in the Koszul modules and spectral sequences of its Chevalley–Eilenberg
cdga CE(g). These results will play a key role in the construction and comparison of algebraic
models for spaces and groups in the sections that follow.

Lie algebras; see [93, 97, 110] for background.

Example 11.3. The cdga CE(sol2) from Section 3.7 has generators a (a cocycle) and b with db =
−a ∧b. Since sol2 is solvable but not nilpotent, Proposition 11.2 implies that CE(sol2) admits no
positive-weight decomposition; one verifies this directly by noting that wt(a ∧b) = wt(a) + wt(b) >
wt(b) = wt(db) for any assignment of positive weights.
Remark 11.4. The positive-weight decomposition furnished by (3)⇒(1) is in general non-canonical
and need not be compatible with the Hirsch extension tower of CE(g). Positive Hirsch weights—
assigning weight k to the generators dual to grk g—are preserved by dCE if and only if g is filtered-
formal, that is, g  gr(g) as filtered Lie algebras; see [110].
A nilpotent Lie algebra that is not filtered-formal admits a positive-weight decomposition on
its CE complex, but not one induced by the Hirsch tower; see [110, Ex. 9.5] for a concrete 5-
dimensional example. Compare also the computational data in Section 11.6.
We now refine the above result in the case of low nilpotency class.
Proposition 11.5. Let g be a finite-dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra. The cdga CE(g) admits a
positive-weight decomposition with weights on g∨concentrated in {1, 2} if and only if g is 2-step
nilpotent.

The preceding discussion shows that positive weights on CE(g) reflect the nilpotency structure
of g, and in the 2-step case yield a particularly simple grading. This grading induces a compatible
structure on the Koszul complex, leading to a tractable theory of Koszul modules.

Remark 11.10. The identification B1(A)/mB1(A)  (Z(g))∨from Lemma 11.9(2) will be applied in

11.5. 1-formality for 2-step nilpotent Lie algebras. Let g be a finite-dimensional nilpotent Lie
algebra over a field of characteristic 0, and set A = CE(g). By a theorem of Hasegawa [52], A is
never formal unless g is abelian; equivalently, the only formal nilmanifolds are tori. Nevertheless,
Chevalley–Eilenberg complexes of nilpotent Lie algebras often exhibit various degrees of partial
formality. We record a simple sufficient condition for 1-formality in the case of 2-step nilpotent Lie
algebras.
Proposition 11.11. Let g be a finitely generated, 2-step nilpotent Lie algebra, and let A = CE(g). If
the cup product map ∪: V2H1(A) →H2(A) is surjective, then A is 1-formal.
Proof. Let M1 = M1(H∗(A)) be the 1-minimal model of H∗(A). By construction, M1 =  V(W1 ⊕
W2), d with W1 = H1(A) and d(W2) = ker(∪: V2W1 →H2(A)). Thus
H2(M1)  V2H1(A)/ ker(∪)  im(∪).
If ∪is surjective, then H2(M1)  H2(A). The natural map ρ: M1 →A induces an isomorphism
in degree 1 and an injection in degree 2 by construction, hence an isomorphism in degree 2 as well.
Therefore ρ is a 1-quasi-isomorphism, and A is 1-formal.
□
Remark 11.12. The surjectivity hypothesis in Proposition 11.11 is neither necessary nor sufficient
for 1-formality in general. The higher Heisenberg algebras h(n) with n ≥2 are 1-formal yet the
cup product is not surjective (Lemma 4.8), while h(1) has vanishing cup product and is not 1-
formal. Thus surjectivity of the cup product and 1-formality are independent conditions in the
2-step nilpotent setting.
11.6. Examples and computational aspects. The following examples illustrate the range of be-
havior of B1(CE(g)) across nilpotent Lie algebras of small dimension. For 2-step algebras, the
module is graded and well-controlled, while for higher nilpotency classes, B1(A) can be non-
homogeneous, non-reduced, or exhibit more complicated scheme-theoretic structure.
Table 2 summarizes the Koszul modules B1(CE(g)) and B1(H∗(CE(g))) for all non-abelian nilpo-
tent Lie algebras of dimension ≤5. All entries have been verified using Macaulay2 [50].
Several patterns emerge from the data:
• For 2-step nilpotent algebras (L3,2, L4,2, L5,4, L5,8), the module B1(A) is always supported
at the origin, with AnnS B1(A) = m, consistent with Theorem 11.18.
• For higher nilpotency classes, the annihilator may be strictly larger than m (as in L5,9) or
non-homogeneous (as in L5,6), while R1(A) = {0} continues to hold.
• The column for B1(H∗(A)) records when the cohomology algebra detects positive-dimen-
sional resonance, which occurs precisely when the cup product V2H1(A) →H2(A) is not
identically zero, and the support is always a union of linear subspaces.
These observations provide supporting evidence for Conjecture 11.24, which predicts that the
resonance scheme of B1(H∗(A)) is always reduced, with support a union of linear subspaces, even
when the scheme Supp B1(A) itself may be non-reduced.

11.7. The Koszul spectral sequence for nilpotent Lie algebras. We now examine how nilpotency
of g forces the Koszul spectral sequence of CE(g) to collapse after finitely many pages. This provides
a uniform bound on where higher-order formality obstructions can appear.
Proposition 11.16. Let g be a k-step nilpotent Lie algebra (γk+1g = 0) and A = CE(g). In the
cohomological Koszul spectral sequence (E•,•

any cochain involving such brackets is zero. Therefore, for r ≥k + 1, the cochain ωA ∪ar = 0, and
the cohomology class dr(α) = [ωA ∪ar] = 0. Since α was arbitrary, dr = 0 for all r ≥k + 1.
□
Remark 11.17. Proposition 11.16 places an upper bound on the pages where formality obstructions
can appear: for a k-step nilpotent Lie algebra g, all nontrivial differentials in the weight spectral
sequence of K•(CE(g)) are concentrated in pages E2 through Ek. This is consistent with the partial
formality theory for nilmanifolds developed later in Section 17.5: a nilmanifold associated to a k-
step nilpotent g can fail to be q-formal for q ≤k −1, but no new obstructions can appear beyond
page Ek. In the 2-step nilpotent case, collapse at E2 means all obstructions are detected by d1 alone,
as in the Heisenberg example of Section 4.6.
11.8. Resonance for nilpotent Lie algebras. This subsection establishes a strong rigidity prop-
erty for resonance of Chevalley–Eilenberg cdgas of nilpotent Lie algebras: all higher-depth reso-
nance varieties either vanish identically or are empty. In particular, in contrast with the resonance
of the cohomology algebra, the resonance of CE(g) exhibits no positive-dimensional components.
This provides a sharp contrast between infinitesimal data encoded by CE(g) and that coming from
H∗(CE(g)), and clarifies the limitations of the tangent cone theorem in the nilpotent setting.
Theorem 11.18. Let g be a finite-dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra over a field k of characteristic
0, and let A = CE(g). Then Ri,s(A) is either {0} or empty, for all i, s ≥1.

11.10. Rigidity of resonance for Chevalley–Eilenberg algebras. We now isolate a rigidity phe-
nomenon for resonance associated to Chevalley–Eilenberg cdgas of nilpotent Lie algebras. This
result may be viewed as an algebraic counterpart of [64, Thm. 1.3], which establishes the same con-
clusion for the group-theoretic resonance varieties of a finitely generated nilpotent s-stage formal
group, using topological methods. The present approach is entirely algebraic, working at the level
of cdga models, and combines the vanishing of resonance for CE(g) established in Theorem 11.18
with the tangent cone equality provided by q-formality via Theorem 9.17.
Theorem 11.23. Let g be a finite-dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra over a field k of characteristic
0, and set A = CE(g). If A is q-formal, then
Ri H∗(A) ⊆{0},
for all i ≤q.
Proof. By Theorem 11.18, Ri(A) ⊆{0} for all i ≥1. Since A is q-formal, Theorem 9.17 gives
Ri H∗(A) = TC0

for all i ≤q. Since Ri(A) ⊆{0}, its tangent cone at 0 is contained in {0}, hence Ri(H∗(A)) ⊆{0} for
all i ≤q.
□
Together, Theorems 11.18 and 11.23 give a complete picture of resonance for CE complexes of
nilpotent Lie algebras at the level of varieties: Ri(CE(g)) is always concentrated at the origin, and
q-formality forces Ri(H∗(CE(g))) to be concentrated there as well for i ≤q.
At the scheme level, however, the picture is more subtle. The scheme Spec S/ AnnS Bi(A) may
be highly non-reduced even when its underlying variety is just the origin, as already seen in Exam-
ples 11.15 and 11.13: in the former, AnnS B1(A) = m2 defines a non-reduced double point, while in
the latter, AnnS B1(A) = (x2, x4, x2
1) is a non-reduced point on the x1-axis. This non-reducedness re-
flects the non-formality of CE(g) in those cases, and is consistent with the scheme-level refinement
of the Tangent Cone Theorem 9.13.


## Page 97


KOSZUL MODULES, HOLONOMY, AND RESONANCE
97
For the cohomology algebra H∗(A), one might hope for better behavior. Non-reduced resonance
schemes at the origin are not, however, exclusively a non-formality phenomenon: they can arise
even for cohomology algebras of formal spaces. For instance, when A = H∗(X) is the exterior
Stanley–Reisner ring of a path on 4 vertices, the Fitting scheme of R1(A) has an embedded non-
reduced component at the origin even though the support variety is reduced [5, Ex. 5.3]. What
does appear to be special to CE complexes of nilpotent Lie algebras is the concentration of the
support at the origin—by Theorems 11.18 and 11.23, the support is always {0}, in sharp contrast
to cohomology algebras of non-formal spaces where positive-dimensional components can appear
away from the origin.
Computational evidence from the classification of low-dimensional nilpotent Lie algebras sug-
gests that for 2-step nilpotent g, the resonance scheme of H∗(CE(g)) is in fact always reduced.
Conjecture 11.24. Let g be a finite-dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra over k, and set A = CE(g).
(i) The resonance scheme Spec S/ AnnS B1(H∗(A)) is reduced.
(ii) Its support R1(H∗(A)) is a union of linear subspaces of H1(A).
In particular, the non-reduced scheme structure that may appear in Supp B1(A)—as illustrated by
the examples in Table 2—never propagates to B1(H∗(A)).
12. The first Koszul module and its presentation
In this section we focus on the first Koszul module B1(A) = H1(K•(A)) of a connected cdga
(A, dA) with dim A1 < ∞. This module plays a distinguished role: it admits an explicit presentation
in terms of the linear and quadratic data of A, fits into an infinitesimal analogue of Crowell’s exact
sequence from group theory, and governs the degree-1 resonance varieties of A. These features
make B1(A) the basic algebraic invariant connecting Koszul homology, resonance, and holonomy
Lie algebras. In particular, the results of this section provide the algebraic backbone for the identifi-
cation of B1(A) with the infinitesimal Alexander invariant of the holonomy Lie algebra and for the
extraction of holonomy Chen ranks from Koszul homology modules.
12.1. A presentation for the S -module B1(A). We begin by giving an explicit presentation of
B1(A) as a module over S = Sym(H1(A)), making transparent its dependence on the linear dif-
ferential dA and the quadratic multiplication on A. This presentation generalizes the one given in
[79, 87] in the case when dA = 0. Unlike the quadratic case, this presentation separates the contri-
butions of the linear differential and the quadratic multiplication, making explicit how non-formal
features enter B1(A) through their interaction. This presentation will serve as the starting point for
the comparison with holonomy Lie algebras and for the analysis of resonance in degree 1.

13. The holonomy Lie algebra of a cdga
In this section, we develop the structure theory of holonomy Lie algebras associated to connected
cdgas, extending the classical quadratic framework to the non-formal setting. In particular, we
express the universal enveloping algebra U(h(A)) in terms of functorial constructions on quadratic
and quadratic–linear algebras, and analyze its associated graded algebra via Koszul duality and the
Poincar´e–Birkhoff–Witt theorem. We also show that the Lie algebra h(A) functorially determines
the 1-minimal model of (A, d), and thus encodes its infinitesimal homotopy invariants.
13.1. Holonomy Lie algebra. In what follows, L(V) will denote the free Lie algebra on a finite-
dimensional k-vector space V, graded by bracket length. We identify L1(V) = V and L2(V) = V ∧V
via the standard correspondence [v, w] ↔v ∧w.
Let (A, dA) be a connected k-cdga with dimk A1 < ∞. Set Ai = (Ai)∨, and denote by
(13.1)

and
dA : A1 −→A2
the multiplication and differential, respectively. Their linear duals combine into a single map

which simultaneously encodes the linear and quadratic relations defining h(A), yielding a quadratic–
linear presentation. Following [65, 88], we make the following definition.
Definition 13.1. The holonomy Lie algebra of A is the quotient

By construction, h(A) is a finitely presented Lie algebra with generators in degree 1 and relations
in degrees 1 and 2. The linear relations come from the image of d∨

are dual to the multiplication in A. Its abelianization satisfies

Proposition 13.2. For any finite-dimensional Lie algebra g over k, there is a canonical isomorphism

Proof. Set A = CE(g) = (V g∨, dCE). Since g is finite-dimensional, A1 = (A1)∨= (g∨)∨ g and
A2 = (A2)∨ V2 g. Under these identifications, we compute ∇A = d∨

For ξ ∈g∨, the Chevalley–Eilenberg differential satisfies (dCEξ)(x, y) = −ξ([x, y]g), so the dual
map gives d∨

gives h(A) = L(g)/⟨im(∇A)⟩ g.
□
13.2. Holonomy Lie algebras under weight conditions. In the presence of weight structures, the
holonomy Lie algebra behaves in a more rigid fashion than in the general quadratic–linear setting.
In particular, positivity of weights allows the linear and quadratic components of the defining re-
lations to be separated, leading to a compatible grading on h(A). Moreover, under suitable weight
restrictions on the differential, holonomy is stable under passage from a cdga to its cohomology
algebra in low weights. The present subsection records these two complementary phenomena.
Proposition 13.3. Let (A, dA) be a connected 1-finite cdga with positive weights. Then the holonomy
Lie algebra h(A) is graded by weight, with grading induced from the positive weight decompositions
of A1 and A2 via the map ∇A = d∨

13.4. Lower central series and enveloping algebra. Although h(A) is defined from the cohomo-
logical grading of A, it need not inherit a grading compatible with bracket length. The discrepancy
is measured by the lower central series filtration.
The lower central series of a Lie algebra g is defined inductively by γ1g = g and γp+1g = [γpg, g].
It is readily seen that each γpg is an ideal of g and that [γpg, γqg] ⊂γp+qg. The associated graded
Lie algebra gr(g) =
L
p≥1 γpg/γp+1g is generated in degree 1, with grading given by bracket length.
When g is already graded, gr(g) = g; otherwise, the two may differ substantially.
Example 13.8. Let (A, d) be the cdga with A = V(a, b), d(a) = 0, and d(b) = −a ∧b. Then
h(A) = sol2 = L(x, y)/([x, y] −y), yet gr(h) = h/h′ = kx is strictly smaller than h, reflecting that the
non-homogeneous relation identifies nontrivial commutators with lower-degree elements.
Example 13.9. Let A = (V(a1, . . . , a5), d) with d(a4) = a1a3 and d(a5) = a1a4 + a2a3. As shown
in [110, Ex. 10.5], the holonomy Lie algebra h(A) is not isomorphic to its associated graded gr(h(A))
under the LCS filtration.
The lower central series filtration on a Lie algebra g induces a natural increasing filtration on its
universal enveloping algebra U(g), defined by

Thus, the associated graded algebra of U(g) recovers the enveloping algebra of the graded Lie

13.6. Koszul cdgas and holonomy ranks. For a general cdga (A, d), the associated graded al-
gebra gr U(h(A)) is governed by the quadratic algebra qql(A, d); when d = 0, this reduces to the
usual situation of Koszul duality for graded algebras. This allows one to extend several classical
consequences of Koszul duality to the non-formal setting.
Definition 13.11. Let (A, d) be a connected cdga with dimk A1 < ∞. We say that (A, d) is quadrat-
ically Koszul if the quadratic algebra qql(A, d) is Koszul (i.e., its trivial module admits a linear free
resolution).
This condition depends only on the quadratic part of the multiplication and differential of (A, d),
and is automatic when d = 0 and A is a Koszul algebra.
Example 13.12. Let A = CE(sol2) = (V(a, b), d) with da = 0 and db = −a ∧b, as in Section 3.7.
The differential introduces a linear relation via d∨(b∨) = a∨, so that ql(A, d) = T(a, b)/⟨a, ab⟩. The
quadratic truncation discards the linear relation, giving qql(A, d) = T(a, b)/⟨ab⟩, a monomial qua-
dratic algebra with a quadratic Gr¨obner basis; hence qql(A, d) is Koszul. Thus (A, d) is quadratically
Koszul.
This example illustrates that quadratic Koszulness depends only on qql(A, d) and is insensitive to
the linear part of the differential: replacing d by 0 gives qql(A, 0) = qA = T(a, b)/⟨ab⟩, the same
algebra.
When (A, d) is quadratically Koszul, the Hilbert series of qql(A, d) controls the holonomy ranks
via Koszul duality, yielding a concrete and computable formula.

13.7. The 1-minimal model from holonomy. The holonomy Lie algebra is not merely an invari-

decomposable differential and quasi-isomorphic to A in degrees ≤1.

The relationship between the holonomy Lie algebra of a cdga and its Sullivan minimal model
is particularly transparent in degree 1. In this setting, the quadratic information encoded by the
holonomy Lie algebra controls the 1-minimal model via the Chevalley–Eilenberg construction and
the lower central series.
The following result is due to Papadima–Suciu [88, Thm. 5.4]. Although stated and proved there
in a purely algebraic framework, it should be viewed as a generalization of earlier results on Malcev
completions and quadratic models due to Bezrukavnikov [9], Bibby–Hilburn [11], and Berceanu–
M˘acinic–Papadima–Popescu [8].
If A is a 1-finite cdga with holonomy Lie algebra h = h(A), there exist natural morphisms
ρn : CE(h/γn(h)) →A, functorial in A, encoding the n-step truncations of the 1-minimal structure.
Theorem 13.16 ([88]). If A is a 1-finite cdga, then c
CE(h(A)) is a 1-minimal model for A, with
structure map ρ: c
CE(h(A)) →A.
In other words, for any 1-finite cdga A, the completed Chevalley–Eilenberg algebra of its holo-
nomy Lie algebra provides a canonical 1-minimal model of A. This construction is functorial and
captures precisely the quadratic part of the rational homotopy type encoded in the 1-minimal model.
As shown in [88], this theorem recovers, in a self-contained and functorial way, a result from [8],
which in turn generalizes work from [9]; see also [11] for a concise summary.

In general, these two effects do not cancel, and the resulting discrepancy detects non-1-formality

However, in certain cases—most notably for Sasakian manifolds of dimension 2n+1 with n > 1—
these opposing effects cancel at the quadratic level, and one has h(A)  h(H∗(A)), even though the
space is not formal. In such situations, the obstruction to formality lies beyond the quadratic level
and is not detected by holonomy.

14. Infinitesimal Alexander invariants and holonomy Chen ranks
Building on the analysis of the first Koszul module B1(A) from Section 12, we relate this mod-
ule to the infinitesimal Alexander invariant B(h(A)) = h′(A)/h′′(A) of the holonomy Lie algebra.
This identification provides a bridge between commutative-algebraic invariants of (A, d) and Lie-
theoretic invariants of h(A), and leads to effective formulas for the holonomy Chen ranks of a con-
nected cdga. The main result of this section establishes Theorem B from the Introduction. In partic-
ular, we obtain infinitesimal analogues of classical results of Massey relating Alexander invariants
and Chen ranks for finitely generated groups.

In general, though, B(g) is an ungraded module.
Example 14.3. Let g = sol2 = ⟨x, y | [x, y] = y⟩. Here V = g/g′ = k¯x and S = Sym(V) = k[x],
while g′/g′′ = ky. The S -module structure is given by x · y = [x, y] = y, so (x −1) · y = 0. Hence,
B(g)  k[x]/(x −1). This module is not graded with respect to any positive grading on S . We will
come back to this computation from a different viewpoint in Example 14.10.
For a connected cdga A, we apply this construction to g = h(A), the holonomy Lie algebra
introduced in Section 13.7, giving the infinitesimal Alexander invariant B(A) B B(h(A)). The case
when g is a finitely generated, quadratic Lie algebra was first considered in [79], and further studied
in [108, 111]; in this case, B(g) is a finitely generated graded S -module.
The construction is functorial: a morphism φ: g →¯g induces an S -linear map B(φ): B(g) →
B(¯g), where B(¯g) is viewed as an S -module via restriction of scalars along the induced map S =
Sym(g/g′) →Sym(¯g/¯g′).

Remark 14.9. Proposition 14.8 shows that the isomorphism B1(A)  B(h(A)) is natural with re-
spect to base change along cdga morphisms. However, the two sides of this isomorphism carry
different information under morphisms, and this distinction is mathematically significant.
For a morphism φ: A →
¯A, the induced map h(φ): h( ¯A) →h(A) is determined entirely by
φ1 : A1 →¯A1 and φ2 : A2 →¯A2: the dual maps (φ1)∨and (φ2)∨determine the map on generators
and relations of the holonomy Lie algebras. In particular, h(φ) is insensitive to the components φk
for k ≥3, and to higher cohomological data.
By contrast, the morphism B1(φ): B1( ¯A) ⊗¯S S →B1(A) is defined via the full chain map
K•(φ): K•(A) →K•( ¯A) of Proposition 8.1, which uses all components of φ. Thus, even when h(φ)
is an isomorphism—for instance, when φ1 is an isomorphism—the map B1(φ) may carry additional
information not visible to h(φ).
The commutativity of the naturality diagram in Proposition 14.8 is therefore a non-trivial com-
patibility statement: it says that the extra data in B1(φ) is always consistent with the data in h(φ),
but the two maps are defined by different portions of φ and need not agree as maps of S -modules
when ¯S = S .
This asymmetry is the source of the obstruction to 1-formality of group homomorphisms devel-
oped in Section 20: when φ models a group homomorphism α: G →H and α∗is the identity on
H1, the map h(φ) is forced to be the identity, but B1(φ) may differ from the identity, obstructing
1-formality of α (Proposition 20.6).

Viewing the graded algebra A as a module over the exterior algebra E = V A1, Theorem 6.4,
together with Proposition 14.12, gives a concrete homological description of the holonomy Chen
ranks: they count the linear strand of the minimal free E -resolution of A.
Corollary 14.13. For all n ≥2,
θn(A) = dimk TorE
n−1(A, k)n.
Taken together, the preceding results give a clear picture of the holonomy Chen ranks. The first
rank is always θ1(A) = dim H1(A), while for n ≥2, the ranks are controlled by the linear strand
of the minimal free E -resolution of A. In particular, by Corollaries 6.5 and 6.6, once a Chen rank
θn(A) vanishes for some n ≥2, all higher ranks vanish as well. This reflects the general principle
that the linear strand of a minimal free resolution cannot revive after terminating, so in that case the
sequence of holonomy Chen ranks eventually stabilizes at zero.
Example 14.14. Let A = V(a, b, c) with da = db = 0 and dc = −a ∧b. Then H1(A) = ka ⊕kb, so
A1 = kx ⊕ky and S = k[x, y]. The Koszul module is B1(A)  S/(x, y)  k, concentrated in internal
degree 0, so Hilb(gr(B1(A)), t) = 1.
The holonomy Lie algebra h = h(A) has generators x, y, z with relations z −[x, y] = [x, z] =
[y, z] = 0. Eliminating z shows h is generated by x, y, with h′ generated by [x, y] and h′′ = 0. Hence
h/h′  k2 and h′/h′′  k, giving holonomy Chen ranks θ1(A) = 2, θ2(A) = 1, and θn(A) = 0 for
n ≥3.
This agrees with Corollary 14.13, since viewing A as a graded E = V(a, b)-module, the linear
strand of the minimal free E -resolution of A contributes a single copy of k in bidegree (1, 2).

Part IV. Spaces, groups, and applications
Having developed the algebraic framework for cdgas and their associated Koszul modules, reso-
nance varieties, and holonomy Lie algebras, we now turn to the topological context, and extend the
constructions of Parts I–III to spaces and groups.
The guiding principle of this part is that many classical invariants of spaces and groups—such
as Alexander invariants, Chen ranks, and their higher-order analogues—admit effective linearized
models governed by the cohomology algebra and its associated Lie-theoretic structures. We show
how these invariants arise naturally from equivariant (co)chain complexes of universal abelian cov-
ers, and how they can be analyzed using the same Koszul and holonomy machinery developed
earlier.
This perspective allows us to compare algebraic and topological invariants systematically, clarify
the role of minimality and formality assumptions, and provide a conceptual framework for applica-
tions to specific classes of spaces and groups, developed in the final sections of this part.
15. The equivariant spectral sequence and its linearization
This section recalls and reformulates the equivariant spectral sequence of Papadima and Suciu
[82], emphasizing its role as a bridge between the cohomology algebra of a space and its homologi-
cal Alexander-type invariants. The spectral sequence arises from the I-adic filtration on equivariant
(co)chains of the universal abelian cover and provides a systematic mechanism for extracting infor-
mation about H∗(Xab; k) from the cup-product structure on H∗(X; k).
We present a cohomological version of the equivariant spectral sequence, adapted to our conven-
tions, and explain the interpretation of its higher differentials in terms of iterated Massey products.
Although this cohomological display is convenient for identifying the differentials, the passage to
the corresponding homological spectral sequence is essential: it is only after changing variance
that the spectral sequence abuts to the homological Alexander invariants Bi(X; k) = Hi(Xab; k).
In this sense, the equivariant spectral sequence provides a topological manifestation of the same
cohomology–homology duality principles that govern the algebraic invariants of groups and their
models, and serves as the main tool for the linearization and comparison results developed in the
sections that follow.
15.1. The cohomological equivariant spectral sequence. Throughout this section, X will be a
connected CW-complex, with fundamental group G. We will denote by Gab = G/[G,G] the abelian-
ization of this group.
Lemma 15.1. Assume Gab = H1(X; Z) is finitely generated. Let Λ = k[Gab] and I ⊂Λ be the
augmentation ideal, where k is a field of characteristic 0.

Remark 15.7. The equivariant spectral sequence associated to the I-adic filtration need not con-
verge in general. As shown by Papadima–Suciu [82], non-convergence may occur when working
over the group algebra kG of a non-residually nilpotent group, since the I-adic topology on kG is
then not Hausdorff. In the present setting, we work with the universal abelian cover Xab and the
group algebra Λ = k[Gab] over a field k of characteristic 0. Although the I-adic filtration on Λ may
fail to be Hausdorff when Gab has torsion (Lemma 15.1(2)), the proof of that lemma shows that the
torsion contribution is confined to filtration degree 0: the ideal IH satisfies IH ⊂Ip for all p ≥1, so
F pC•(Xab; k) = IpC•(Xab; k) is unaffected by the torsion for p ≥1. Hence the spectral sequence,
which is determined by the filtration quotients F p/F p+1 for p ≥0, converges to the associated
graded of the completed equivariant cohomology under the finite-type assumption on X.

We conclude this subsection by placing the equivariant spectral sequence in its broader literature
context.
Remark 15.15. Liu–Maxim–Wang [63] independently establish that all higher differentials in the
cohomological equivariant spectral sequence of [82] are computed by iterated Massey products
along [ωX] ∈H1(X; k), working over arbitrary field coefficients, consistent with Theorem 5.1(3).
From the homological perspective adopted here, the same information is encoded by iterated appli-
cations of the coalgebra structure ∇X on H∗(X; k), naturally compatible with Koszul homology.
Remark 15.16. A closely related spectral sequence, originating with Novikov [76] in the de Rham
setting and developed further by Farber [45] and Pajitnov [78], is studied systematically by Kohno
and Pajitnov [57]. Given a finite CW-complex X, a representation ρ: G →GL(n, C), and a class
α ∈H1(X; C), they construct a spectral sequence starting from H∗(X; ρ) and converging to the coho-
mology H∗(X; γgen) with coefficients in the generic deformation γt(g) = et⟨α,g⟩ρ(g). The differentials
are given by iterated Massey products with α, paralleling the structure of the higher differentials in
Theorems 5.1 and 15.3. When ρ is the trivial representation, their spectral sequence degenerates for
formal spaces by the classical DGMS argument [31], yielding ker Lα/ im Lα  H∗(X; γgen).
The present paper may be viewed as the Koszul-theoretic counterpart of this circle of ideas: work-
ing over an arbitrary k-cdga with the trivial representation, we replace the exponential deformation
by the canonical element ωA, and gain an explicit S -module structure on the abutment governing

Alexander-type invariants. The equivariant spectral sequence provides a conceptual bridge from
the cohomology ring H∗(X; k) to the homological Alexander invariants Bi(X; k) = Hi(Xab; k), but
it does not, by itself, yield explicit or functorial algebraic models for these invariants. To pass
from this topological framework to computable objects, one must linearize the equivariant theory
and compare it with algebraic constructions arising from k-cdga models. This requires two addi-
tional steps: first, replacing equivariant (co)chains by their associated graded objects with respect
to the augmentation filtration; and second, passing to suitable completions to control convergence
and functoriality. The outcome is a precise identification between Alexander invariants and Koszul
homology modules, developed in the next section.
16. Alexander invariants and infinitesimal models
In this section we recall the classical Alexander invariants of a space and introduce their infin-
itesimal analogues arising from Koszul modules. These infinitesimal invariants provide linearized
and functorial models for Alexander modules, expressed entirely in terms of the cohomology alge-
bra and the associated Koszul differential. We show that, after suitable completion, the classical and
infinitesimal theories are canonically identified at the level of associated graded modules, under min-
imality and finite-type hypotheses on X. The main result of this section establishes Theorem C(1)
from the Introduction. This comparison clarifies the role of Koszul modules as algebraic avatars of
Alexander invariants and prepares the ground for the Chen ranks computations that follow.
16.1. Alexander invariants. Let X be a connected CW-complex, with a single 0-cell chosen as
basepoint x0. Set G = π1(X, x0) and Gab = G/[G,G]. The group algebra Λ = k[Gab] is a commuta-
tive Noetherian ring, since Gab is a finitely generated abelian group. Lifting the cellular structure to
the maximal abelian cover Xab, we obtain an augmented chain complex of free Λ-modules,

16.2. Infinitesimal Alexander invariants. We now introduce the infinitesimal Alexander invari-
ants, defined via the Koszul modules of the cohomology algebra H∗(X; k), and then extend the
construction to arbitrary cdga models. Unlike the classical literature, which focuses primarily on
the first Alexander invariant B1(X), the Koszul formalism naturally produces invariants in all de-
grees. The results below show that these higher infinitesimal invariants model, after completion and
linearization, the full tower of Alexander modules Bi(X).
Definition 16.2. Let X be a connected CW-complex with dimk H1(X; k) < ∞. The infinitesimal
Alexander invariants of X (over k) are the Koszul homology modules
Bi(X; k) B Bi

possible, we suppress k from the notation.
If X has finitely many i-cells, then Bi(X) is a finitely generated S -module. In particular, if X is
a finite-type CW-complex, then all infinitesimal Alexander invariants Bi(X) are finitely generated
S -modules.
Note that H∗(X; k) with zero differential is a cdga, but it is a model for X only when X is formal.
The definition above is thus purely cohomological and requires no formality assumption; formality
will only enter when comparing Bi(X) with the classical Alexander invariants Bi(X).
More generally, let (A•, dA) be a connected k-cdga with dimk H1(A) < ∞. Recall from Section 3.3
the construction of the Koszul chain complex K•(A) and its homology modules

which are graded modules over S = Sym(H1(A)). If H∗(A) is of finite type, then each Bi(A) is a

the infinitesimal Alexander invariants Bi(X; k) defined above.
We may also consider the modules Bi(A) associated to partial cdga models. If A is a q-model for
a q-finite CW-complex X, the Koszul modules Bi(A) are finitely generated S -modules for all i ≤q,
but they may depend on the choice of model. Unlike the classical Alexander invariants, the Koszul
modules Bi(A) associated to a cdga model need not be homotopy invariants of X. This dependence
on the chosen model is intrinsic and already appears for formal spaces.
Example 16.3. Let X = S 1. This is a formal space, modeled by its cohomology algebra, H∗(X; k) =
V(a) with deg(a) = 1, for which B1(X; k) = 0. But X is also modeled by the cdga A = CE(sol2)
from Section 3.7, for which B1(A)  k[x]/(x −1).
Remark 16.4. One could similarly define cohomological Alexander invariants Bi(X; k) = Hi(Xab; k)
and their infinitesimal analogues via Koszul cohomology. While such invariants fit naturally into the
equivariant cohomological spectral sequence, they play no role in the group-theoretic applications
considered here, which are inherently homological in nature.
The infinitesimal Alexander invariants Bi(X; k) should be viewed as linearized counterparts of
the classical Alexander invariants Bi(X; k). A precise comparison between the two theories requires
additional structure, and will be carried out in the two subsections that follow. Under minimality
and finite-type hypotheses on the CW-structure of X, the associated graded of the equivariant chain
complex is canonically isomorphic to the Koszul complex of H∗(X; k), and this upgrades to a filtered
quasi-isomorphism of completions.

lar chain complex (C∗(X; Z), ∂) and boundary maps ∂q : Cq(X; Z) →Cq−1(X; Z). We also consider
the corresponding chain and cochain complexes with coefficients in a field k, denoted (C∗(X; k), ∂X)
and (C∗(X; k), δX).

C•(Xab; k),

The following elementary observation, due to Papadima–Suciu [82, Lem. 3.2], identifies this

Lemma 16.5. With respect to the I-adic filtration:
(1) grI(∂ab) = ∂X ⊗idS , so grI C•(Xab; k)  (C•(X; k) ⊗k S, ∂X ⊗idS ).
(2) grI(δab) = δX ⊗idS , so grI C•(Xab; k)  (C•(X; k) ⊗k S, δX ⊗idS ).
Proof. Part (1) is the specialization of [82, Lem. 3.2] to the k[G]-module M = Λ, where Λ = k[Gab].
Under the identification Cq(Xab; k) = Λ ⊗k Cq(X; k), the I-adic filtration is given by Fn = In ⊗k
C•(X; k), and the claim follows by passing to associated graded. Part (2) follows at once.
□
Lemma 16.5 shows that the associated graded equivariant chain complex is obtained from the
ordinary cellular chain complex (C•(X; k), ∂X) by extension of scalars from k to S . In particular,
the degree-0 differential in the I-adic filtration (equivalently, the d0 differential in the equivariant
spectral sequence) is induced by ∂X.
Thus, the cellular boundary maps of X control the zeroth-order behavior of the equivariant dif-
ferential ∂ab. Eliminating this contribution greatly simplifies the associated graded complex, and
motivates the following definition.
Definition 16.6. A connected, finite-type CW-complex X is called minimal if its cellular boundary
maps vanish, i.e.,
∂q : Cq(X; Z) −→Cq−1(X; Z)
vanishes for all q ≥1.
Equivalently, for every q ≥0, the number of q-cells of X is equal to the Betti number bq(X) =
rank Hq(X; Z). In view of Lemma 16.5, this condition is equivalent to
grI(∂ab) = 0,
that is, the equivariant boundary operator has no term of degree 0 with respect to the I-adic filtration.
If X is a finite-type, minimal CW-complex, then C∗(X; Z)  H∗(X; Z) as chain complexes, and in
particular each Hq(X; Z) is a free abelian group. Consequently, the abelianization Gab = H1(X; Z)
is free abelian, the group algebra Λ = k[Gab] is a Laurent polynomial algebra, and after tensoring
with k,
C•(X; k)  H∗(X; k)
as graded vector spaces. Minimality plays a threefold structural role in what follows. First, it
implies that Gab is free abelian, so that Λ is a Laurent polynomial algebra and the I-adic filtration is
Hausdorff. Second, it ensures that the linearization of the equivariant coboundary operator δab—its

associated graded map with respect to the I-adic filtration—is realized by an actual map of filtered
complexes (Theorem 16.9). Third, under the finite-type hypothesis, it guarantees that cochain–chain
duality commutes with I-adic completion degreewise.
Minimal CW-complexes arise in a variety of geometric contexts. Basic examples include spheres,
tori, and closed orientable surfaces, all endowed with their standard CW-structures. Complements
of complex hyperplane arrangements also admit minimal CW-structures, as shown by Dimca–
Papadima [34] and Randell [94].
Minimality is preserved under finite wedges and under products (with the product CW-structure)
of finite-type, minimal CW-complexes.
The following examples illustrate what goes wrong in the absence of minimality.

16.4. Linearization theorem and Alexander invariants. We now describe the classical lineariza-
tion results for Alexander-type invariants of minimal CW-complexes. The key idea is that, under
minimality, the I-adic filtration on the equivariant (co)chain complex linearizes the boundary maps,
and the resulting linearized complex is canonically isomorphic to the Koszul complex of the coho-
mology algebra. This is the associated-graded version of the Papadima–Suciu theorem [82].
Theorem 16.9 ([82, Thm. 12.6]). Let X be a connected, finite-type, minimal CW-complex. There is

17. Algebraic models for spaces
In this section we develop the rational homotopy-theoretic framework needed to relate Koszul
modules to topological invariants of spaces. After recalling minimal and finite-type cdga models
and their functorial properties, we examine formality and partial formality through the behavior of
Koszul complexes. In particular, we introduce a spectral-sequence obstruction to q-formality arising
from positive weight decompositions.
We then apply this machinery to complete the linearization program initiated in Section 16.
Specifically, we establish part (2) of Theorem C by identifying completed Alexander invariants with
completed Koszul homology. Finally, we reinterpret resonance varieties and tangent cone phenom-
ena in terms of finite-type cdga models, highlighting the role of Koszul techniques in both formal
and non-formal settings.
17.1. Minimal models. A connected cdga (M , d) is minimal if M = V V is free as a graded
algebra and the differential is decomposable, that is, dM ⊂M + ·M +. Equivalently, M is obtained
from k by a sequence of Hirsch extensions,

ρ: M (A) →A, unique up to isomorphism [113, 73]. For each q ≥1 there is also a q-minimal model,
generated in degrees ≤q, well-defined up to q-isomorphism. A cdga (A, dA) is formal (respectively
q-formal) precisely when M (A) (resp. Mq(A)) is quasi-isomorphic (resp. q-quasi-isomorphic) to

Conversely, any such tower arises from a unique 1-minimal cdga. This construction provides an
algebraic description of 1-minimal cdgas in terms of towers of nilpotent Lie algebras.
17.2. Algebraic models for spaces. Given a space X, we let A•
PL(X) be the commutative differential
graded Q-algebra of rational polynomial forms, as defined by Sullivan in [113]. There is then a
natural isomorphism H∗(APL(X))  H∗(X, Q) under which the respective induced homomorphisms
in cohomology correspond.
Now let k be a field of characteristic 0, and q a positive integer. We say that a k-cdga (A, d) is a
q-model (or simply a model) over k for X if A is q-equivalent (or weakly equivalent) to APL(X) ⊗Q k.
Finite-type cdga models are essential in what follows, since all resonance and support loci are
defined via finitely generated Koszul complexes.
If f : X →Y is a map between two spaces admitting k-cdga models AX and AY, we say that a cdga
morphism φ: AY →AX is a q-model (or model) for f if it is q-equivalent (or weakly equivalent) to
APL( f) ⊗Q k. A functorial model is a functor X { AX assigning to each continuous map f : X →
Y a cdga morphism A( f): AY →AX which models f in the above sense. Functorial q-models
are defined analogously. By considering classifying spaces K(G, 1) for groups G, we may speak
about (functorial) 1-models for groups and group homomorphisms. For instance, if X is a smooth
manifold, then Ω•
dR(X), the de Rham algebra of smooth forms on X, is a model of X over R. This
model is functorial: if f : X →Y is a smooth map, then f ∗: Ω•
dR(Y) →Ω•
dR(X) is a cdga morphism
that models f.
A rational homotopy equivalence between X and Y is a zig-zag of maps (going either way)
connecting them via rational quasi-isomorphisms. Thus X and Y have the same rational homotopy
type precisely when such a zig-zag exists. By functoriality, a rational quasi-isomorphism f : X →Y
induces a quasi-isomorphism APL( f): APL(Y) →APL(X). Consequently, the weak isomorphism type
of APL(X) depends only on the rational homotopy type of X. As another consequence, the existence
of a finite model for a space X is an invariant of rational homotopy type. All these notions have
q-analogues; in particular, whether X admits a q-finite q-model depends only on its rational q-
homotopy type.
Every connected space X admits a minimal model M (X), defined as a minimal model for APL(X).
This is a minimal cdga, unique up to isomorphism, equipped with a quasi-isomorphism ρ: M (X) →
APL(X). Thus the isomorphism type of M (X) depends only on the rational homotopy type of X.
Moreover, if A is a rational cdga model for X, then there is a quasi-isomorphism M (X) →A
corresponding to ρ via the chosen weak equivalence between A and APL(X). Similarly, for each
q ≥1, the space X admits a q-minimal model, Mq(X), equipped with a q-quasi-isomorphism
ρq : Mq(X) →APL(X).
17.3. Formality of spaces. A space X is formal (over a field k of characteristic 0) if A•
PL(X) ⊗Q k
is weakly equivalent to (H∗(X; k), 0). By the discussion from Section 2.1, formality over Q is
equivalent to formality over k. Partial formality is defined similarly, and q-formality over Q is
equivalent to q-formality over k.
Formality is preserved under products, wedges, and retracts. Examples of formal spaces include
rational cohomology spheres and tori, compact connected Lie groups and their classifying spaces,
Eilenberg–MacLane spaces K(π, n) for n ≥2, and, famously, every compact K¨ahler manifold [31].


## Page 134


134
ALEXANDER I. SUCIU
Formality plays a central role in relating cohomology-level resonance to the finer invariants arising
from cdga models.
The following theorem of Papadima and Yuzvinsky [90] relates certain properties of the minimal
model of a space X to the Koszulness of its cohomology algebra.
Theorem 17.1 ([90]). Let X be a connected space with finite Betti numbers.
(1) If M (X)  M1(X), then the cohomology algebra H∗(X, Q) is a Koszul algebra.
(2) If X is formal and H∗(X, Q) is a Koszul algebra, then M (X)  M1(X).
Consequently, if X is formal, then X is rationally aspherical if and only if H∗(X, Q) is a Koszul
algebra. As an application of Theorem 17.1, we have the following formality criterion.
Corollary 17.2 ([80]). Let X be a connected, finite-type CW-complex, and suppose that H∗(X, Q) is
a Koszul algebra. Then X is 1-formal if and only if X is formal.
17.4. A Koszul spectral sequence obstruction to formality. The goal of this subsection is to
introduce a computable obstruction to partial formality, formulated in terms of the weight filtration
on the Koszul complex of a finite-type cdga model. Unlike classical obstructions based on Massey
products or minimal models, this obstruction operates at the level of Koszul homology and detects
failures of q-formality through the non-collapse of a natural spectral sequence.
Theorem 17.3. Let X be a path-connected space admitting a finite-type cdga model (A, d) with
positive weights. If X is q-formal, then the weight spectral sequence of K•(A) collapses at E1 in
total degrees ≤q + 1, i.e., E2  E∞in those degrees. Consequently, any nonzero differential dr
(r ≥2) with p + q′ ≤q + 1 in the weight spectral sequence of K•(A) implies that X is not q-formal.
Proof. Since X is q-formal, (A, d) is q-quasi-isomorphic to (H∗(X; k), 0). By Theorem 8.6(2), the q-
quasi-isomorphism induces isomorphisms grm Bi(A)  grm Bi(H∗(X; k)) for all i ≤q, giving Hilbert
series equality in that range. By Theorem 7.14, this forces collapse of the weight spectral sequence
at E1 in those total degrees.
□
The relevance of Theorem 17.3 to q-formality comes from the following observation. If X is
q-formal, then any finite-type cdga model (A, d) with positive weights is q-quasi-isomorphic to the
cohomology algebra (H∗(X; k), 0). It follows that the corresponding Koszul complexes have the
same Hilbert series in total degrees ≤q + 1. By Corollary 7.15, this forces the weight spectral
sequence of K•(A) to collapse at E1 in that range. Therefore, the existence of a nonzero higher
differential in these degrees obstructs q-formality.
Theorem 17.3 shows that q-formality forces all higher differentials dr (r ≥2) in the weight
spectral sequence to vanish in the relevant range. The situation for the first differential d1—that is,
the Koszul differential of H∗(A) on the E1-page—is more subtle: it carries information about the
cup-product structure of H∗(X; k) rather than about the homotopy type, and may be nonzero even for
formal spaces and formal models. For instance, the graphic configuration spaces Conf(E, Γ) of [8]
are 1-formal for triangle-free Γ, yet the weight spectral sequence of the Bibby model has nonzero
d1; see Section 21.6. This shows that nonvanishing of d1 is not a homotopy-invariant obstruction
in general, and depends on the choice of model. The following question asks for which models it
nevertheless detects non-formality.
Question 17.4. For which finite-type cdga models (A, d) with positive weights does nonvanishing
of d1 in the weight spectral sequence of K•(A) imply non-1-formality of the underlying space? Is

17.7. Resonance varieties of spaces. Let X be a connected, finite-type CW-complex over a field k
of characteristic 0. Classically, the resonance varieties of X are defined in terms of its cohomology
algebra, endowed with the zero differential:
(17.4)
Ri,s(X, k) B Ri,s H∗(X; k).
One may also consider the corresponding resonance support loci,
(17.5)
Ri,s(X, k) B Ann VsBi(H∗(X; k), k).
Both are homogeneous algebraic subvarieties of the affine space H1(X; k) and depend only on the
homotopy type of X. For simplicity, we will abbreviate Ri(X, k) = Ri,1(X, k) and Ri(X, k) =
Ri,1(X, k). Since X is connected, one has R0(X, k) = R0(X, k) = {0}. Therefore, by Theorem 9.7,
R1(X, k) = R1(X, k).
Resonance varieties defined in this way have been extensively studied and play a central role in
the topology of spaces and groups; see, for instance, [37, 83, 85, 87, 100, 101, 104]. They enjoy
strong naturality properties with respect to homotopy equivalences and finite regular covers, and
may be viewed as infinitesimal counterparts of characteristic and Alexander varieties in the theory
of cohomology jump loci. In this paper, we concentrate on their description via Koszul complexes
and finite-type cdga models.
When X is formal (or, more generally, q-formal), the cohomology algebra H∗(X; k) provides a
q-model for X, and the above definition captures the relevant infinitesimal structure. In contrast,
for non-formal spaces, cohomology alone no longer suffices. One may replace the Sullivan model
APL(X) ⊗Q k by a finite-type k-cdga model (A, d), and consider the resonance varieties Ri,s(A) of
this model. These sets are not homotopy invariants in general, nor are they canonically defined as
global algebraic varieties; however, their analytic germs at the origin are homotopy invariants.
For any i-finite k-cdga model A of X, the tangent cone at the origin to Ri,s(A) is contained in
Ri,s(X, k), by Theorem 9.13. In particular, it suffices to assume that A is a 1-finite model to obtain
this containment in degree i = 1—a hypothesis that holds for all finitely generated fundamental
groups.
This comparison motivates the study of resonance varieties of cdgas as infinitesimal approxima-
tions to classical resonance, and leads to the purely algebraic tangent-cone problem addressed in
Section 9.3. Throughout the paper, we distinguish between resonance varieties defined from coho-
mology algebras and those arising from i-finite k-cdga models. The varieties themselves depend on
the choice of model and are not homotopy invariants in general (see Examples 5.5, 8.3, and 9.10 for
the model CE(sol2) of the formal space S 1); however, their analytic germs at the origin are homo-
topy invariants [35], and these germs coincide with the germs of the classical resonance varieties
Ri,s(X, k).

Theorem 17.12 ([37, 35]). Let X be a q-formal space (over C). For all i ≤q and all s, the following
hold.
(1) The germ of V i,s(X, C) at the identity 1 coincides with the germ of Ri,s(X, C) at the origin
0.
(2) Each irreducible component of Ri,s(X, C) is a rationally defined linear subspace.
The property (2) has proved to be an effective tool for detecting non-formality of groups and
spaces, even in situations where classical obstructions (Massey products, higher homotopy groups,
etc.) are difficult or impossible to compute directly. The linearity phenomenon underlying Theo-
rem 17.12(2) is not a formal consequence of the tangent cone identification in (1). A conceptual
explanation is provided by the Exponential Ax–Lindemann theorem of Budur–Wang [18], which
shows that analytic germ equivalence under the exponential map forces cohomology jump loci to
be algebraic subtori, and hence resonance components to be rationally defined linear subspaces.
More generally, if X admits a finite-type cdga model (A, d) over C, the germ V i,s(X, C)(1) is
analytically isomorphic to Ri,s(A)(0) [35]. Conjecture 9.18 should thus be viewed as a purely alge-
braic analogue of these germ-equality and local linearity phenomena, formulated entirely in terms
of Koszul complexes and Hirsch extensions, and without reference to global jump loci.

17.9. Resonance varieties of closed manifolds. Let Mm be a closed, connected, orientable smooth
manifold, and let k be a field of characteristic 0. By Poincar´e duality, the cohomology algebra
H∗(M; k) is a PDm-algebra, with orientation determined by the fundamental class [M].
In favorable situations, the manifold M admits a finite-type PDm-cdga model A whose cohomol-
ogy is H∗(M; k). When such a model exists, all the results from Section 4 apply functorially to M.
In particular:
• the Aomoto cohomology groups satisfy the palindromic duality

• the resonance and support loci associated to M obey corresponding Poincar´e-duality con-
straints.
These symmetry phenomena are formal consequences of Poincar´e duality and do not require
formality of the manifold. Thus, whenever a finite-type model exists, resonance varieties are subject
to strong duality constraints, even in the presence of nontrivial Massey products.
Manifolds without finite-type models. In general, closed manifolds do not admit finite-type cdga
models. This phenomenon already occurs in dimension 3: while every closed, orientable 3-manifold
has a PD3 cohomology algebra, its minimal model need not be of finite type. In such situations, the
resonance varieties of H∗(M; k) are often the only accessible cohomological jump loci, and one
must work directly at the level of the cohomology algebra. This approach is particularly effective in
dimension 3, where the PD3 structure is completely encoded by the alternating 3-form
(17.10)
µM : V3H1(M; k) −→k,
µM(a ∧b ∧c) = ⟨a ∪b ∪c, [M]⟩,
as shown by Sullivan and further developed in [102].
Formal manifolds. At the opposite extreme lie formal manifolds, such as compact K¨ahler man-
ifolds and complements of complex hyperplane arrangements. In these cases, resonance varieties
are entirely determined by the cohomology algebra, and a rich theory has been developed exploiting


## Page 141


KOSZUL MODULES, HOLONOMY, AND RESONANCE
141
this fact. From the point of view of this paper, the use of H∗(M; k) in the formal setting reflects not
a limitation, but rather the absence of higher-order homotopical obstructions.
Non-formal manifolds with finite-type models. Between these two extremes lies a broad and ge-
ometrically rich class of manifolds that are typically non-formal, yet always admit finite-type mod-
els. This class includes nilmanifolds, quasi-projective manifolds, Sasakian manifolds, and Seifert
fibered spaces. It is precisely for such manifolds that the Koszul-type techniques developed in this
paper are most effective, as they capture higher-order information not visible at the level of coho-
mology alone.
For instance, if G is a finitely generated nilpotent group, then the associated nilmanifold admits
a finite-type Chevalley–Eilenberg model A = CE(g), where g = Lie(G ⊗k). Results of M˘acinic [64]
show that partial formality imposes strong constraints on truncated cohomology and resonance,
while allowing the full cohomology algebra to exhibit genuinely higher-degree phenomena.
Further applications of resonance theory to manifolds admitting finite-type models will be ex-
plored in the final sections of this paper, focusing on quasi-projective manifolds (Section 21) and on
Sasakian manifolds and Seifert fibered spaces (Section 22).
18. Algebraic models for groups
Associated to a finitely generated group G are several Lie algebras capturing progressively dif-
ferent levels of structure. These include intrinsic graded Lie algebras arising from the lower central
and derived series, filtered Lie algebras arising from Malcev completion, and quadratic Lie algebras
constructed from group cohomology.
More concretely, the main objects are the graded Lie algebra gr(G) associated to the lower central
series, the filtered Malcev Lie algebra m(G), and the quadratic holonomy Lie algebra h(G). The
purpose of this section is to recall these constructions and the comparison maps between them, and
to explain how formality properties govern when these Lie algebras coincide.
18.1. Lower central series. The lower central series (LCS) of a group G is defined by γ1(G) = G
and γn+1(G) = [γn(G),G]. This is an N-series, in the sense of Lazard [61], meaning [γn(G), γm(G)] ⊆
γn+m(G), and each γn(G) is normal in G. The quotients γn(G)/γn+1(G) are abelian and central in
G/γn+1(G). The associated graded Lie algebra of G is
(18.1)
gr(G) B
M
n≥1
γn(G)/γn+1(G),
with bracket induced by the group commutator. Over a field k of characteristic 0, gr(G; k) = gr(G)⊗Z
k is a graded Lie algebra over k. Both assignments G 7→gr(G) and G 7→gr(G; k) are functorial.

18.5. Formality properties of groups. A finitely generated group G is 1-formal if a classifying
space K(G, 1) is 1-formal. This property depends only on G = π1(X) for any path-connected space
X, and is preserved under finite free products and direct products of finitely generated groups. In
particular, the free groups Fr are 1-formal, since K(Fr, 1) = Wr(S 1) is formal, for all r ≥1.
The following lemma (which follows from the definitions) provides a useful 1-formality criterion.
Lemma 18.5. Let G be a finitely generated group. Suppose there is a 1-formal group K and a homo-
morphism φ: G →K such that φ∗: H1(K; Q) →H1(G; Q) is an isomorphism and φ∗: H2(K; Q) →
H2(G; Q) is injective. Then G is also 1-formal.
Example 18.6. If G is a finitely generated group with b1(G) equal to 0 or 1, then G is 1-formal.
Indeed, the claim is true for K0 = {1} and for K1 = Z. Moreover, if b1(G) = i ∈{0, 1}, we may
define a homomorphism φ: G →Ki satisfying the assumptions of Lemma 18.5. Therefore, the
claim holds for G, too.
The 1-formality property of a finitely generated group G depends only on its Malcev Lie algebra,
m(G), or its rationalization, GQ. Indeed, G is 1-formal if and only if m(G) is isomorphic, as a filtered
Lie algebra, to the degree completion of a finitely generated quadratic Lie algebra; see e.g. [21, 68].

We recall from [110, Thm. 6.5] a useful criterion for filtered-formality. For completeness, we
include a proof of the part of the result that will be needed later on.
Let (M •, d) be a minimal cdga generated in degree one, endowed with the canonical filtration
{Mi}i≥0 from (17.1), where each sub-cdga Mi given by a Hirsch extension of the form Mi−1 ⊗k
V(Vi). The underlying cga M • possesses a natural set of positive weights, which we will refer
to as the Hirsch weights: simply declare Vi to have weight i, and extend those weights to M •
multiplicatively. We say that the cdga (M •, d) has positive Hirsch weights if the differential d is
homogeneous with respect to those weights. If this is the case, each sub-cdga Mi also has positive
Hirsch weights.
Proposition 18.8 ([110]). Let G be a finitely generated group. If the canonical 1-minimal model
M (G; k) is filtered-isomorphic to a 1-minimal model with positive Hirsch weights, then G is filtered-
formal over k.
Proof. Let M be a 1-minimal model with positive Hirsch weights. By homogeneity of the differ-
ential, the dual Lie algebra L(M ) is graded and its completion is isomorphic to the Malcev Lie
algebra m(G; k) by Theorem 18.9. Thus m(G; k) is the completion of a graded Lie algebra, which is
precisely the definition of filtered-formality.
□
Proposition 18.7 shows that the various notions of formality can be detected purely at the level
of associated graded and Malcev Lie algebras. These Lie-theoretic descriptions become particu-
larly effective in the presence of a 1-finite 1-model, whose existence and basic properties will be
developed in Section 18.7.
18.6. Minimal models and CE-complexes. We now explain how Malcev Lie algebras give rise
to canonical 1-minimal cdga models via Chevalley–Eilenberg constructions. This correspondence
provides the main bridge between the Lie-theoretic invariants of finitely generated groups and the
cdga-based resonance theory developed later in the paper. In particular, it allows us to interpret
resonance varieties purely in terms of filtered Lie algebra data.

tent Lie algebras
m(G)  L(M1(G)).
Thus the assignments G 7→m(G) and (M1, d) 7→L(M1) define adjoint functors between finitely
generated groups (via their Malcev Lie algebras) and 1-minimal cdgas.

Theorem 18.12. Let G be a finitely generated, torsion-free nilpotent group, let X be the associated
compact nilmanifold, and let A = CE(g) be the Chevalley–Eilenberg cdga of its Malcev Lie algebra
g. Then, for every i ≥0, the analytic germ of Ri(A) at 0 is trivial.
Proof. By Nomizu’s theorem, the Sullivan minimal model of the nilmanifold X is given by the
Chevalley–Eilenberg cdga CE(g) of its Malcev Lie algebra g. Since G is torsion-free nilpotent, g is
nilpotent, and CE(g) is a finite-type Sullivan model.
By Theorem 11.18, the resonance varieties Ri(CE(g)) are either empty or equal to {0}, for all i.
Consequently, the analytic germ of Ri(CE(g)) at 0 is trivial.
□
