18.7. Groups with 1-finite 1-models. The purpose of this subsection is to single out a class of
finitely generated groups for which rational homotopy–theoretic methods yield effective and com-
putable invariants. Throughout, we fix a field k of characteristic 0.
The next theorem provides a precise description of the Malcev Lie algebra of a group admitting
a finite-type model in low degrees.
Theorem 18.13 ([88]). Let G be a finitely generated group that admits a 1-finite 1-model (A, d)
over k. Then its Malcev Lie algebra satisfies
m(G; k)  bh(A),
the lower central series completion of the holonomy Lie algebra of A.
Thus, a finitely generated group admits a 1-finite 1-model if and only if its Malcev Lie algebra
is the lower central series completion of a finitely presented Lie algebra, generated in degree 1 with
relations in degrees 1 and 2.
We now explain how the spectral obstructions to 1-formality introduced earlier translate, in the
case of groups, into precise Lie-theoretic conditions on the Malcev Lie algebra. In particular,
we show that degeneration of the Koszul weight spectral sequence in low degrees is equivalent
to quadraticity of m(G), thereby recovering 1-formality from the viewpoint of algebraic models.
Theorem 18.14. Let G be a finitely generated group admitting a 1-finite 1-model (A, d) with positive
weights. If the weight spectral sequence of K•(A) degenerates at the E1-page in total degree ≤2,
then m(G; k) is quadratic. In particular, G is 1-formal.
Proof. By Theorem 18.19, the degeneration hypothesis implies h(A)  h(H∗(A)), so h(A) is qua-
dratic. By Theorem 18.13, m(G; k)  d
h(A), hence m(G; k) is the completion of a quadratic Lie
algebra, and G is 1-formal.
□
For a 1-formal group G, the cohomology algebra (H∗(G; k), 0) provides a 1-finite 1-model.
However, this construction is not functorial with respect to arbitrary homomorphisms (see Exam-
ple 20.1). Canonical functorial models, such as Sullivan’s APL or de Rham algebras, exist for all
spaces, but are almost never 1-finite. The existence of a 1-finite 1-model is therefore a strong and
subtle condition, independent of formality.
This condition can be obstructed at the level of minimal Sullivan models. The following finiteness
result from [88] provides a basic necessary criterion.
Theorem 18.15 ([88]). Let X be a space admitting a q-finite q-model, and let Mq(X) denote its
q-minimal Sullivan model. Then dimk Hi(Mq(X)) < ∞for all i ≤q + 1.
As an immediate consequence, if a finitely generated group G is either finitely presented or admits
a 1-finite 1-model, then the second Betti number of its 1-minimal model is finite.
This criterion excludes large and natural families of groups. In particular, if π is a finitely gen-
erated, very large group (i.e., admits a free, non-cyclic quotient) and G = π/π′′ is its maximal
metabelian quotient, then G admits no 1-finite 1-model [88]. In particular, free metabelian groups
Fn/F′′
n (n ≥2) do not belong to this class.
On the other hand, the existence of a 1-finite 1-model is not implied by finite presentability.
Using Alexander polynomials together with a refined form of the Tangent Cone Theorem 17.12, due
to Budur and Wang [18], one can construct finitely presented groups—indeed, fundamental groups


## Page 148


148
ALEXANDER I. SUCIU
of closed, orientable 3-manifolds—which admit no 1-finite 1-model; see [103] and the examples
discussed there. These examples reflect a general obstruction principle: failure of rational linearity
for tangent cones to characteristic varieties in degree 1 precludes the existence of a 1-finite 1-model.
From this point on, our default setting will be that of finitely generated groups admitting a 1-finite
1-model. Within this framework, classical results for 1-formal groups appear as special cases, while
genuinely new phenomena arise from the presence of a nontrivial differential. In particular, formal-
ity assumptions will be viewed as conditions under which the general model-theoretic constructions
simplify or degenerate.
18.8. Holonomy–associated graded comparison in the Carnot case. We consider the compari-
son between holonomy Lie algebras of 1-finite 1-models and the associated graded Lie algebra of
a finitely generated nilpotent group. Such a comparison is not available in general, but becomes
canonical under strong rigidity assumptions, in particular in the Carnot (graded) setting described
below.
Proposition 18.16. Let G be a finitely generated nilpotent group with Malcev Lie algebra g =
m(G) ⊗Q k, and set A = CE(g). Assume that g is Carnot graded, i.e., g  gr(g) as graded Lie
algebras. Then there is an isomorphism
ΦA : h(A) −→gr(G; k).
Proof. Since A = CE(g), Proposition 13.2 gives a canonical isomorphism h(A)  g. Moreover, since
m(G) ⊗Q k  g as filtered Lie algebras, taking associated graded yields gr(G; k)  gr(g). Under the
Carnot assumption, the grading on g identifies it canonically with its associated graded: g  gr(g).
Combining these identifications yields
h(A)  g  gr(g)  gr(G; k).
The resulting isomorphism ΦA is obtained by composing the above identifications; it is not canonical
and depends on the choice of Carnot grading on g.
□
In general, the construction above is not functorial in G or A, and therefore does not yield a
canonical comparison map between h(A) and gr(G; k) beyond the Carnot setting. This motivates the
following conjectural lifting property.
Conjecture 18.17. Let G be a finitely generated group admitting a connected 1-finite 1-model (A, d)
with positive weights such that im(d∨) is concentrated in weights ≥2. Under these hypotheses,
Lemma 13.5 provides a natural epimorphism ΞA : h(H∗(A)) ↠h(A). We conjecture that there exists
an epimorphism of filtered Lie algebras
ΦA : h(A) −↠gr(G; k)
such that ΦG = ΦA ◦ΞA.
Remark 18.18. The conjecture can be reformulated as the inclusion ker(ΞA) ⊆ker(ΦG). In the
Carnot case, the conjecture holds by Proposition 18.16, and all holonomy and graded objects in-
volved are isomorphic.

18.9. A Koszul criterion for quadraticity. The identification of the infinitesimal Alexander in-
variant B1(A) with B(h(A)) from Theorem 14.7 allows one to translate homological information
about the Koszul complex K•(A) into structural constraints on the holonomy Lie algebra. In partic-
ular, degeneration of the weight spectral sequence in low degrees imposes strong restrictions on the
presentation of h(A).
We record next a criterion ensuring that h(A) is quadratic, expressed purely in terms of the Koszul
complex of A.
Theorem 18.19. Let A = M1(G) be the 1-minimal Sullivan model of a finitely generated group G,
equipped with positive Hirsch weights. If the weight spectral sequence of K•(A) degenerates at the
E1-page in total degree ≤2, then h(A)  h(H∗(A)); in particular, h(A) is quadratic.
Proof. Since A has positive Hirsch weights, H1(A) = V∨
1 is concentrated in weight 1, so h(A) is
positively graded and generated in weight 1 (Proposition 13.3). For a positively graded Lie algebra
generated in weight 1, the LCS-associated graded coincides with h(A) itself: grLCS(h(A)) = h(A).
By Theorem 18.13, d
h(A)  m(G; k). For a finitely generated positively graded Lie algebra L with
separated filtration, grLCS(bL) = grLCS(L). Applying Quillen’s isomorphism (18.7) therefore gives
h(A) = grLCS(h(A))  grLCS(m(G; k))  gr(G; k).
On the other hand, the degeneration hypothesis implies B1(A)  B1(H∗(A)) (by Theorem 7.5 and
Proposition 7.4), and hence B(h(A))  B(h(H∗(A))) (by Theorem 14.7). By Lemma 13.5, there
is an epimorphism ΞA : h(H∗(A)) ↠h(A), which is an isomorphism on B-modules. Since h(A) 
gr(G; k) and h(H∗(A)) = h(G; k), the epimorphism ΞA has the same source and target as ΦG, and
B(ΞA) being an isomorphism forces ker(ΞA) ⊆h(G; k)′′. Since h(A) has the same Hilbert series as
gr(G; k)  h(A), and ΞA is a graded surjection between algebras of the same Hilbert series, ΞA is an
isomorphism. Hence h(A)  h(H∗(A)) is quadratic.
□
18.10. Finitely generated, torsion-free nilpotent groups. Let G be a finitely generated, torsion-
free nilpotent group. By Malcev theory, G admits a rational Malcev completion m(G), a finite-
dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra over Q. Moreover, the Eilenberg–MacLane space K(G, 1) is a
nilmanifold, and Nomizu’s theorem identifies the rational cohomology of G with the Lie algebra
cohomology of m(G),
(18.15)
H∗(G; Q)  H∗(m(G)).
Consequently, the Chevalley–Eilenberg cdga CE(m(G)) provides a rational 1-model for G, functo-
rial with respect to group homomorphisms. Since the Chevalley–Eilenberg differential preserves
the grading by bracket length, CE(m(G)) admits a positive-weight decomposition. As a result, the
Koszul complex K•(CE(m(G))) carries a natural weight filtration, giving rise to a spectral sequence
of the type considered in Theorem 17.3.
An Eilenberg–MacLane space K(G, 1) for such a group G admits a canonical realization as a
nilmanifold M = Rn/G, where n = dim m(G). In this setting, CE(m(G)) is not only a rational
1-model for G, but in fact a finite-type Sullivan model for the nilmanifold M itself. In particular,
CE(m(G)) computes the full rational cohomology of M and provides a common algebraic model for
both M and its fundamental group.
Finitely generated, torsion-free nilpotent groups thus form a natural class of examples for the
techniques developed in this paper. Their classifying spaces admit canonical finite-type models with

positive weights, making them particularly well suited for the Koszul spectral sequence methods of
Sections 7.2 and 17.4.

the cdga CE(m(G)) admits a positive-weight decomposition. This induces a splitting of the lower
central series filtration on m(G), yielding a filtered Lie algebra isomorphism

gr(m(G)).
By definition, this identifies G as a filtered-formal group.

The higher Heisenberg groups provide a concrete illustration of the interaction between filtered-
formality, graded-formality, and the Koszul spectral obstruction introduced in Section 17.4. For
n ≥1, the nth Heisenberg group H(n) is defined by the presentation
(18.17)
H(n) = ⟨a1, . . . , an, b1, . . . , bn | [ai, bi] = [aj, bj], [ai, aj] = [ai, bj] = [bi, bj] = 1 for i , j⟩.
Each group H(n) is torsion-free, 2-step nilpotent, with infinite cyclic center. Its Malcev Lie algebra
is the higher Heisenberg Lie algebra h(n), generated by xi, yi, z with relations [xi, yi] = z.

H(1) is not 1-formal, while H(n) is 1-formal for all n > 1.

Remark 18.22. The examples above suggest a close relationship between the structure of a nilpo-
tent Lie algebra and the degree of partial formality of the associated space.
For the Heisenberg groups H(n), and more generally for Heisenberg-type groups with defining
2-form of rank 2m [64], the Lie algebra is 2-step nilpotent, yet the corresponding nilmanifold is
(m −1)-formal but not m-formal. From the perspective of the Koszul spectral sequence, the failure
of q-formality is detected by a nontrivial differential whose source lies in total degree q+1, reflecting
the first appearance of relations beyond quadratic data in the Chevalley–Eilenberg differential.
More generally, while the nilpotency class alone is too coarse to control q-formality, the present
results indicate that finer invariants—such as the weights and ranks appearing in CE(g)—play a
decisive role via the associated Koszul spectral sequence. A systematic investigation of these rela-
tionships will be pursued elsewhere.

Remark 18.24. A precursor of Theorem 18.23 appears in work of Carlson and Toledo [21], who
proved the vanishing of degree-one resonance for 1-formal nilmanifolds. The result above may be
viewed as a higher-degree refinement, made possible by the Koszul spectral sequence techniques
developed in this paper.
19. Chen ranks, Koszul modules, and resonance
In this section we study Chen ranks of groups through the lens of 1-finite 1-models and their
associated Koszul modules. In the 1-formal setting, resonance is a purely cohomological invariant,
and Chen ranks are controlled by the holonomy Lie algebra. Beyond formality, however, resonance
acquires genuinely differential features, and the relationship between Chen ranks and resonance be-
comes more subtle. We show that Koszul modules provide the correct infinitesimal object governing
Chen ranks in both settings, while simultaneously clarifying the limitations of purely cohomological
approaches. The main result of this section establishes Theorem D from the Introduction.
The results of this section explain how vanishing and linearized resonance constrain the asymp-
totic behavior of Chen ranks and place conjectural Chen ranks formulas within a unified Koszul-
theoretic framework. This perspective provides a conceptual bridge between topological formu-
lations of the problem and their algebraic incarnations, and suggests a natural testing ground for
extending Chen ranks phenomena beyond the classical 1-formal setting.

19.3. Vanishing resonance for groups admitting a 1-finite 1-model. This subsection treats the
extreme case where the first resonance variety is trivial. Although this case lies at one end of the
spectrum from the perspective of the Chen ranks conjecture, it already exhibits the key mechanism—
finite length of Koszul modules—that governs asymptotic Chen ranks behavior. The results of this
subsection are based on the general principle established in Proposition 9.20, which relates trivial
resonance support for a finite-type cdga model to finite length of the corresponding Koszul modules,
under a mild spectral sequence collapse condition.

may equivalently be formulated in terms of resonance varieties.

The following example shows that the two hypotheses in Corollary 19.9 are genuinely different,

Example 19.11. Let G = F2/γ3(F2) be the integral Heisenberg group, and let A = (V(a1, a2, a3), d)

of the model is trivial: R1(A) = {0}. On the other hand, H1(G; k) = k⟨a1, a2⟩, and the cup product
on H1(G; k) vanishes. Therefore, R1(G; k) = H1(G; k).
This example shows that trivial resonance of a 1-model does not imply trivial resonance of the
cohomology algebra. Nevertheless, Corollary 19.9 applies, since the asymptotic Chen ranks are
governed by the Koszul module B1(A), rather than by R1(G; k).

the characteristic variety being trivial, by Theorem 17.12. The results of this subsection show that,

the relevant object controlling finiteness of Koszul modules and eventual vanishing of Chen ranks,
even when the classical tangent cone formula fails.

algebra by resonance of a finite model, allowing one to recover analogous Chen ranks consequences
without assuming 1-formality.
Remark 19.13. Koszul modules with vanishing resonance form a rigid class of algebraic objects
with strong finiteness properties. They arise in several foundational problems of commutative alge-
bra and algebraic geometry, notably Green’s conjecture on syzygies of canonical curves and, more

generally, in the study of syzygies of vector bundles on algebraic varieties [1, 2]. In a different

The results of this subsection show that vanishing resonance is equivalent to finite length of
the associated Koszul modules. This finite-length property is the key mechanism underlying the
eventual vanishing of Chen ranks. See Remark 19.16 for a more general perspective when resonance
is nontrivial.
19.4. Linear resonance, separability, and isotropicity. We now recall the structural conditions
on linear components of the first resonance variety that enter the Chen ranks formulas discussed in
the next subsection. These conditions control both the scheme-theoretic geometry of resonance and
the algebraic structure of the associated infinitesimal Alexander invariants.
Let V = H1(G; k) and let K ⊆V2V be the kernel of the cup-product map V2V →H2(G; k). A
linear subspace L ⊆V is called isotropic if L∧L ⊆K, that is, if the cup product vanishes identically
on L. It is called separable if (L ∧V) ∩K ⊆L ∧L. A subspace L is called strongly isotropic if
it is both isotropic and separable, or equivalently, if (L ∧V) ∩K = L ∧L. For instance, if K = 0,
then every subspace is separable, but the only isotropic subspaces are those of dimension at most 1.
On the other hand, if K = V2V, then all subspaces are isotropic, but no proper, non-trivial subspace
is separable. These examples emphasize that isotropicity is an intrinsic condition on L, whereas
separability reflects how L interacts with the ambient space V.
The relevance of these notions to the geometry of the resonance varieties was clarified in [3].
Let G be a 1-formal group, and let R1(G; k) be its first resonance variety, with the scheme structure
given by the annihilator of B1(G; k). By Theorem 17.12(2), each irreducible component of R1(G; k)
is a (rationally defined) linear subspace. Separability alone already forces strong scheme-theoretic
properties: each separable irreducible component of the projectivized resonance variety is reduced
and isolated [3, Thm. 4.5]. Consequently, if all components are separable, the resonance scheme is
reduced and projectively disjoint [3, Cor. 4.6].
Isotropicity enters in a different way. For an isotropic component L of resonance, reducedness of
the corresponding projective component is equivalent to strong isotropicity [3, Thm. 5.1]. While this
equivalence is not required for Chen ranks formulas themselves, it explains why strong isotropicity
naturally appears when one seeks converse or rigidity statements. These conditions are discussed
from the algebro-geometric perspective of the graded Koszul module W(V, K) in [46].
19.5. The Chen ranks conjecture and its algebraic refinements. To place the following results
in context, we recall the original form of the Chen ranks conjecture, introduced in [99] for funda-
mental groups of complex hyperplane arrangements. The conjecture predicts that, in sufficiently
high degrees, the Chen ranks of an arrangement group G are determined by the geometry of the first
resonance variety R1(G; C) ⊂H1(G; C). Arrangement groups are 1-formal, and hence, by Theo-
rem 17.12, their resonance varieties decompose as finite unions of linear subspaces. In this setting,
the conjecture asserts that the asymptotic behavior of the Chen ranks θn(G) depends only on the
dimensions of these components.
For a general 1-formal group G, the relevance of resonance to Chen ranks is explained by the
comparison between the holonomy Lie algebra and the associated graded Lie algebra of G/G′′. By
Theorem 18.3, there is a natural epimorphism
(19.12)
h(G; k)/h(G; k)′′ −↠gr(G/G′′; k)

which is an isomorphism in the 1-formal case. As a consequence, the Chen ranks of a 1-formal
group are governed by the infinitesimal Alexander invariant, whose structure is reflected in the
geometry of R1(G; k), subject to additional structural constraints on its components.
An algebraic form of the Chen ranks conjecture for 1-formal groups was established in [3],
refining earlier work of Cohen–Schenck [24]. The latter required the irreducible components of
R1(G; k) to be isotropic, reduced, and projectively disjoint—conditions arising naturally in the
quasi-projective setting via [37].
A key observation of [3] is that separability alone already implies both reducedness of the res-
onance scheme and projective disjointness of its components, and hence quasi-projectivity is not
required to state or prove a Chen ranks formula. The subsequent paper [4] strengthens this result by
identifying the effective asymptotic range n ≥b1(G) −1 in which the Chen ranks formula holds, a
substantially more delicate problem.

Remark 19.15. In the absence of strong isotropicity, Chen ranks formulas of the form (19.13) need
not hold, as shown for instance in [80] for right-angled Artin groups and in [24, 111] for the upper
McCool groups.
A key observation of [3] is that separability alone already implies that each irreducible component
of R1(G; k) is reduced and projectively disjoint [3, Thm. 4.5], so the Chen ranks formula does not
require quasi-projectivity once these conditions are satisfied. The subsequent paper [4] refines this
by identifying the effective asymptotic range n ≥b1(G) −1 in which the formula holds.
K¨ahler groups provide a different geometric regime in which separability is known in special
cases (for instance, fundamental groups of complete intersection Kodaira fibrations), while isotrop-
icity fails (except in the vanishing resonance case). In this setting, Chen ranks are conjecturally
governed by a formula of type (19.13), with free groups replaced by surface groups. We refer to [3]
for the precise conjecture and for its verification in the case of compact algebraic surfaces admitting
two independent Kodaira fibrations.
The discussion above takes place entirely within the 1-formal setting, where resonance is a purely
cohomological invariant and the Chen ranks are controlled by the holonomy Lie algebra. Beyond
formality, resonance acquires genuinely differential features, and the relationship between Chen
ranks and resonance becomes more subtle.
For groups admitting a 1-finite 1-model (A, d), Theorem 19.2 shows that the Chen ranks are
determined exactly by the Koszul module B1(A). From this perspective, vanishing resonance corre-
sponds to finite length of B1(A), while the presence of linear resonance suggests that B1(A) should
admit a natural comparison map to the direct sum of the Koszul modules associated to the individual
resonance components.
In the 1-formal case, strong isotropicity of these components provides precisely the condition
needed to ensure that this map is surjective with finite-dimensional kernel. This indicates that any

Remark 19.16. The vanishing-resonance case R1(A) = {0} corresponds to the situation where
the Koszul module B1(A) has finite length; see Section 19.3 for the resulting vanishing of Chen
ranks. This regime plays an important role in several contexts, including Green’s conjecture on
syzygies of canonical curves [1, 2] and the study of Torelli groups and Johnson filtrations [36, 84].
From this perspective, the Chen ranks conjecture can be viewed as a refinement of the finite-length
phenomenon to the case where R1(A) is nontrivial but admits a linear decomposition structure.
Guided by the above principle, one is led to seek a differential condition on (A, d) that governs
the structure of B1(A) in a manner analogous to strong isotropicity in the 1-formal case. The correct
analogue, suggested by the theory of decomposable arrangements [79, 107] and illustrated by the
pure elliptic braid groups in §21.4, is an exact decomposition of B1(A)—rather than merely an
asymptotic one—indexed by the resonance components.

Remark 19.19. The nonvanishing of d1 reflects the presence of essential higher-weight terms in
the differential of a minimal model for X. While this phenomenon is compatible with the existence
of nontrivial triple Massey products, the obstruction above is strictly stronger: it detects non-1-
formality at the level of Koszul modules and Chen ranks, even in situations where Massey products
alone may be inconclusive.
Remark 19.20. In Section 21.3, we show that for the Bibby model A(n) of the elliptic configuration
space Conf(E, n), the differential d1 is nonzero for n ≥3, from which we conclude that P1,n is not
1-formal for n ≥3.
20. Functorial models and formality of maps
In this section we study formality properties of maps and group homomorphisms from the per-
spective of functorial algebraic models. While formality of spaces is detected by the existence of
suitable quasi-isomorphisms between cdga models, formality of maps requires compatibility of such
models with morphisms.
We show that, for finitely generated groups admitting functorial 1-finite 1-models, the holo-
nomy Lie algebra provides a natural Lie-theoretic framework for detecting 1-formality of homomor-
phisms. In particular, functoriality ensures compatibility between Malcev completions, holonomy
Lie algebras, and infinitesimal Alexander invariants, leading to effective obstructions to formality
of maps.
20.1. Formality of maps. We begin by recalling from Section 2.2 the notion of formality for cdga
morphisms and its topological interpretation for continuous maps. Let (A, dA) and (B, dB) be two
formal cdgas. A morphism φ: A →B is formal if and only if the induced map between minimal
models fits into a homotopy-commutative diagram

As shown in [31], every compact K¨ahler manifold is formal; moreover, a holomorphic map
between such manifolds is formal over R. In general, a map between formal spaces need not be
formal. For example, the Hopf fibration S 3 →S 2 is not formal, even though both spaces are
formal.

is not 1-formal.
□
20.5. Alexander invariants as secondary obstructions to formality. We record a secondary ob-
struction to 1-formality of maps arising from the infinitesimal Alexander invariant. Proposition 20.5
shows that failure of naturality of completed Alexander invariants detects non-1-formality of a ho-
momorphism. Proposition 20.6 refines this at the level of Koszul modules: even when a map induces
the identity on H1, nontriviality of the induced morphism on B1 obstructs 1-formality. The concep-
tual reason is that B1(φ) depends on strictly more data than h(φ), as explained in Remark 14.9.
The following proposition exhibits an automorphism invisible to Malcev and associated graded
Lie algebra invariants, but detected by the completed Alexander invariant.

Remark 20.8. Proposition 20.7 shows that 1-formality of a homomorphism cannot be detected
solely at the level of Malcev or associated graded Lie algebras: the automorphism α induces the
identity on both h(G) and gr(G), yet fails to be 1-formal. This contrasts with the situation for IA-
automorphisms of free groups (Example 20.1), where non-formality is already visible at the Malcev
level. The Alexander invariant B1(G) and its Koszul counterpart B1(AG) provide obstructions to
1-formality that are invisible at the level of Malcev Lie algebras. The Bq-formality framework of
§8.5 provides the conceptual setting for these higher-order phenomena.
21. Ordered configuration spaces on an elliptic curve
The purpose of this section is to illustrate how the Koszul-module and spectral-sequence tech-
niques developed earlier can be applied to configuration spaces. For ordered configurations of points
on an elliptic curve, the fundamental group P1,n admits a finite-type cdga model due to Bibby,
whose Koszul invariants are remarkably simpler than those arising from the cohomology algebra.
By comparing these invariants and analyzing the Koszul spectral sequence associated to the weight
filtration, we obtain a conceptual and computable explanation for the failure of 1-formality of P1,n.
21.1. Quasi-projective varieties and cdga models with weights. Let X be a connected, smooth,
complex quasi-projective variety. By a theorem of Deligne, each cohomology group of X carries
a mixed Hodge structure [30]. If M is a smooth projective variety—or, more generally, a compact
K¨ahler manifold—then M is formal, by the theorem of Deligne–Griffiths–Morgan–Sullivan [31].


## Page 167


KOSZUL MODULES, HOLONOMY, AND RESONANCE
167
In general, smooth quasi-projective varieties need not be formal; nevertheless, their rational homo-
topy type is governed by cdga models that carry a positive-weight structure of the type studied in
Section 7.1.
More precisely, every X as above admits a good compactification: a smooth projective variety X
and a normal-crossings divisor D = S
j∈J D j such that X = X \ D. Morgan [73] associates to such
a compactification a rationally defined cdga A(X) = A(X, D), the Gysin model, whose underlying
graded pieces are

21.2. The Bibby model for Conf(E, n). Let E be a smooth elliptic curve over C, and let
(21.3)
Conf(E, n) = {(z1, . . . , zn) ∈E×n | zi , zj for i , j}
be the ordered configuration space of n points on E, with fundamental group
P1,n = π1(Conf(E, n)),
the pure braid group on n strings on the torus. Bibby [10] constructed a finite-type cdga model
(A(n), d) for this space. Building on a spectral sequence of Totaro [116], her construction applies
to complements of unimodular elliptic arrangements in E×n. The Bibby model is a special case of
Dupont’s Orlik–Solomon functorial model [38] for complements of hypersurface arrangements in
smooth projective varieties, and the resulting cdga carries a positive-weight structure compatible
with Definition 7.1.
We now recall Bibby’s especially transparent description of (A(n), d). This model is generated in
degree 1 by elements ai, bi (1 ≤i ≤n) and ei j (1 ≤i < j ≤n), subject to the relations
(ai −a j)eij = (bi −b j)ei j = ei jeik −ei jejk + eike jk = 0,
with differential given by
dai = dbi = 0,
dei j = (ai −a j)(bi −b j).
This model carries a positive-weight structure compatible with the Morgan convention (21.2): the
generators ai, bi have weight 1 and the generators ei j have weight 2, reflecting the bidegrees (1, 0)
and (0, 1) in (21.1) via wt(A(n)p,q) = p + 2q, so that the differential preserves total weight since
dei j = (ai −aj)(bi −bj) lands in weight 1 + 1 = 2. (Deligne’s MHS convention assigns weight 2 to
H1(E), so the two conventions agree up to the re-indexing α 7→3 −α on {1, 2}.)

21.4. Comparison with the pure braid group Pn. The results of the preceding subsections invite
a direct comparison between the pure elliptic braid group P1,n = π1(Conf(E, n)) and its classical
counterpart, the pure braid group Pn = π1(Conf(C, n)). Both are fundamental groups of configura-
tion spaces, but on two different one-dimensional complex algebraic groups: the additive group C
and the elliptic curve E. The contrast between their LCS ranks, Chen ranks, and formality properties
illustrates how the Koszul module framework extends naturally from the 1-formal to the non-formal
setting.

Geometry of the fibration: why Conf(E, n) does not factor. The product formula (21.4) and the fi-
bration tower (21.5) are intimately linked: the fibers C\{k points} have trivial monodromy in homol-
ogy, which is what makes the Leray–Hirsch theorem applicable and forces formality. By contrast,
Conf(E, n) admits no analogous fibration tower whose fibers have trivial monodromy action on their
homology. The natural projection Conf(E, n) →Conf(E, n −1) has fiber E \ {n −1 points}, a punc-
tured torus whose fundamental group is the free group F2(n−1) of rank 2(n −1). The monodromy
of this fibration—the action of π1(Conf(E, n −1)) on H1(E \ {n −1 pts}; k) —is nontrivial, coming
from the translation action of E on itself. This nontrivial monodromy obstructs the Leray–Hirsch
product formula and is the geometric source of non-formality of P1,n. Concretely, the obstruction is
encoded in the Bibby differential: d(ei j) = (ai −aj)(bi −bj) records the monodromy contribution of
the diagonal ∆ij, mixing the two H1(E; k)-directions. In the linear case, the analogous differential is


## Page 172


172
ALEXANDER I. SUCIU
d(ei j) = ai −aj (a single cohomology class), reflecting trivial monodromy; the elliptic case requires
the product (ai −a j)(bi −b j), reflecting the two-dimensional nature of H1(E; k).
Remark 21.5. The non-formality of Conf(E, n) fits into a broader framework: for smooth complex
algebraic varieties whose MHS on cohomology is pure, formality follows from Morgan’s theo-
rem [73]; conversely, Dupont [39] shows that mixed weight structures can obstruct formality. For
hyperplane arrangement complements, the MHS on H∗is pure of Tate type, consistent with their
formality. For Conf(E, n), the compactness of E introduces weight-1 classes from H1(E; k), making
the MHS genuinely mixed and obstructing formality; the Bibby differential d(ei j) = (ai−aj)(bi−bj)
is the explicit cdga manifestation of this mixed structure.
Toward a non-formal Chen ranks conjecture. The P1,n family sits at the intersection of two threads.
On one hand, by Proposition 21.3 and Theorem 13.13, the LCS ranks of P1,n are governed by the
same Orlik–Solomon algebra OS(An−1) as Pn—but modified by the extra 2n generators, reflecting
the two-dimensional fiber directions. On the other hand, the Chen ranks of P1,n follow the same
template as the Chen ranks conjecture for 1-formal groups: with h2 =

As discussed in Section 19.5, this suggests an extension of the Chen ranks conjecture to non-
1-formal groups admitting positive-weight models with linear resonance. The relevant condition
replacing strong isotropicity should be formulated at the level of B1(A): here it is the direct sum de-
composition B1(A(n)) 
L
i<j S/(xi −x j, yi −y j), which follows from the block-diagonal structure
of the Bibby differential and yields the Chen ranks formula directly. The family {P1,n}n≥2 provides
a worked non-formal example of such an extension, and a natural test case for any proposed gener-
alization; see Question 19.17.
21.5. The Koszul module of H≤2(P1,n). In [9], Bezrukavnikov computed presentations of the Mal-
cev Lie algebras m(Pg,n) of the pure braid groups Pg,n for all g, n ≥1. He observed that these groups
are 1-formal for g > 1 and n ≥1, and for g = 1 and n ≤2, and stated without proof that P1,n is not
1-formal for n ≥3. A complete proof of non-1-formality of P1,n for n ≥3 was later given in [37],
using the Tangent Cone Theorem for cohomology jump loci of 1-formal groups and the nonlinearity
of R1(Conf(E, n)).
We provide here an independent obstruction to 1-formality, based on Chen ranks and Koszul
modules, which illustrates a general phenomenon: for spaces admitting finite-type models, discrep-
ancies between the Koszul invariants of the model and those of the cohomology algebra provide
computable obstructions to formality. Concretely, although the Bibby model A(n) and the truncated
cohomology algebra H≤2(Conf(E, n); k) agree in degree 1 and arise from the same fundamental
group, their Koszul modules differ substantially. This discrepancy, reflected in fundamentally dif-
ferent asymptotic growth of the corresponding Chen ranks, is precisely what obstructs 1-formality
of P1,n.

tained from H∗(E×n; k) by quotienting out the classes dual to the diagonals ∆i j = {(z1, . . . , zn) |

where [∆ij] denotes the Poincar´e dual cohomology class of the diagonal ∆i j ⊂E×n. Dualizing the
quadratic relations gives the presentation of the holonomy Lie algebra, which is therefore 2-step
nilpotent.
□

By contrast, consider the graphic configuration spaces Conf(E, Γ) = {(z1, . . . , zn) ∈En | zi ,
zj for all i j ∈Γ} associated to a finite simple graph Γ on n vertices [8]. For a triangle-free graph Γ,
the differential d1([eij]) = [(ai −aj)(bi −bj)] is nonzero in the weight spectral sequence of the Bibby
model of Conf(E, Γ), yet π1(Conf(E, Γ)) is 1-formal by [8]. This shows that nonvanishing of d1 in
the weight spectral sequence of a finite-type model does not in general obstruct 1-formality—the
obstruction must be read from the minimal Sullivan model itself, as in Theorem 17.3, rather than
from an arbitrary positive-weight model; see Question 17.4.
Remark 21.15. The Bibby model applies more generally to unimodular elliptic arrangements,
where the underlying Orlik–Solomon relations are combinatorially determined, but the additional
relations encoding the defining equations of the arrangement are not. This raises the question of to
what extent resonance varieties and formality properties of elliptic arrangement complements are
governed by combinatorial data alone; see [101, Prob. 8.3]. Related questions were formulated in
[10] and remain largely open outside special classes of arrangements.
22. Hirsch extensions, circle actions, and Sasakian geometry
Many of the examples discussed in this paper arise from manifolds equipped with a geometric
circle action. This paradigm is most naturally expressed in the setting of Sasakian manifolds, where
one has a canonical S 1-action whose orbit space carries a K¨ahler orbifold structure.
From the viewpoint of rational homotopy theory, Sasakian manifolds form a particularly well-
behaved class of spaces: they admit explicit finite models encoding both the geometry of the base
and the Euler class of the circle action. These models provide strong constraints on higher homotopy
operations, including sharp bounds on the possible length of Massey products, and allow for a
unified treatment of formality, filtered-formality, and Malcev Lie algebras.
The purpose of this section is twofold. First, we analyze Massey products in 1-step Hirsch
extensions with positive weights, establishing a general vanishing result that applies far beyond
the Sasakian setting. Second, we recall the cdga models associated to circle actions arising from
Sasakian structures, following work of Tievsky, and specialize to orientable Seifert fibered spaces.

Thus, the following are equivalent:
(1) the short exact sequence (22.1) splits in the derived category;
(2) the long exact sequence (22.2) splits into short exact sequences;

For a 1-step Hirsch extension A = H∗(B) ⊗[e]
V(t), triple Massey products in H∗(A) are canoni-
cally associated to this extension class: more precisely, a triple Massey product ⟨α, β, γ⟩is nontrivial
if and only if the corresponding class in B1(A) maps nontrivially under the connecting morphism ∂.
It follows that H∗(A) has nontrivial triple Massey products if and only if the Koszul–Gysin sequence
(22.2) fails to split.
Step 4: Vanishing of higher Massey products. Since A is a 1-step Hirsch extension of a formal cdga
with positive weights, its minimal model inherits a positive weight decomposition supported in two
adjacent weights (the original weights coming from H∗(B) together with the weight of the generator
t). Applying Lemma 22.1, we conclude that all Massey products in H∗(A) of length at least four
vanish.
This completes the proof.
□
Remark 22.3. The preceding proof separates two distinct mechanisms governing Massey prod-
ucts. On the one hand, the Koszul–Gysin exact sequence (22.2) detects the primary extension data
associated to the Hirsch extension, encoded by the connecting morphism ∂. This is precisely the
information responsible for triple Massey products. On the other hand, the vanishing of Massey
products of length at least four is a consequence of the positive weight structure: the Hirsch exten-
sion produces a minimal model supported in two adjacent weights, which prevents the existence of
higher defining systems.
From the viewpoint of the Koszul spectral sequence, the filtration induced by weights has length
two, and the spectral sequence collapses at E2. While this collapse reflects the absence of higher
extension data in the filtered complex, it does not by itself imply the vanishing of higher Massey
products. Rather, it is consistent with the fact that only the primary extension class (detected at
the E2-page) can contribute, in agreement with the appearance of triple—but not higher—Massey
products.

higher-order compatibility of the Lie brackets.
For Sasakian manifolds of dimension 2n + 1, Theorem 22.11 shows that these higher-order dis-
crepancies vanish for n > 1, whereas the case n = 1 retains nontrivial higher-order behavior.
22.3. Sasakian manifolds and partial formality. Let M2n+1 be a compact Sasakian manifold. A
general reference for Sasakian geometry is the monograph of Boyer and Galicki [13]. By a result of
Ornea and Verbitsky [77], any compact Sasakian manifold admits a quasi-regular Sasakian structure,
and we will assume this throughout.
In the quasi-regular case, a basic structural theorem in Sasakian geometry ([13, Thm. 7.1.3])
ensures that M carries an almost free S 1-action, generated by the Reeb vector field. The orbit space
B = M/S 1 is then a compact K¨ahler orbifold of real dimension 2n, with K¨ahler class h ∈H2(B; Q).

Theorem 22.10 ([7]). Every compact K¨ahler orbifold is formal.
Tievsky [115] constructed an explicit Sullivan model for M in terms of the cohomology algebra
of the base. More precisely, let (H∗(B; k), 0) denote the cdga with zero differential, and let ω ∈
H2(B; k) be the cohomology class of the K¨ahler form. Then M admits a finite cdga model of the
form
(22.6)
 H∗(B; k) ⊗k
V(c), d,
deg(c) = 1,
where the differential is determined by d(c) = ω. This model is minimal whenever H∗(B; k) is
generated in degree one; in general, it still serves as a finite Sullivan model for M, sufficient for all
formality considerations below.
From a homotopy-theoretic standpoint, (22.6) is a Hirsch extension of the cdga (H∗(B; k), 0). If
ω = 0, the circle action is rationally trivial and the model splits as a tensor product. If ω , 0, the
differential introduces a single quadratic relation reflecting the Euler class of the fibration. More
precisely, the grading on H∗(B; k) by cohomological degree, together with wt(c) = 1 if ω = 0 and
wt(c) = 2 if ω , 0, defines a system of positive Hirsch weights compatible with the differential.
We now explain how the boundedness of a Hirsch extension forces partial formality. This pro-
vides a purely algebraic proof of the partial formality of compact Sasakian manifolds, originally
established in [88, 54].
Theorem 22.11. Let B be a formal cdga of finite type, concentrated in degrees ≤2n, and admitting
positive weights. Let A = B ⊗e
V(c) be a 1-step Hirsch extension with deg(c) = 1. Then A is
(n −1)-formal. Moreover, if e , 0, then A is not n-formal.
Proof. Since B is formal, there exists a quasi-isomorphism B →(H∗(B), 0). By functoriality of
Hirsch extensions, we may replace B by its cohomology algebra and assume
A =  H∗(B; k) ⊗k
V(c), d,
d(c) = e ∈H2(B).
The minimal model of A is generated in degree 1, and the only nontrivial differential involving
the new generator c is given by d(c) = e. Since H∗(B) is concentrated in degrees ≤2n, every
indecomposable element in total degree ≤n involves at most one occurrence of c.
It follows that, up to degree n, the Sullivan minimal model of A admits a truncation whose
differential agrees with that of the cohomology algebra (H∗(A), 0). More precisely, the relative
minimal model map (VV, d) →(H∗(A), 0) induces an isomorphism on cohomology in degrees
≤n −1 and a monomorphism in degree n. Hence, A is (n −1)-formal.
If e , 0, the failure of the Koszul–Gysin sequence to split yields a nontrivial triple Massey
product in H≤n+1(A), as described in Theorem 22.2. Indeed, since H∗(B) is concentrated in degrees
≤2n, the first degree in which such a triple Massey product can occur is degree n when n ≥2,
and degree 2 when n = 1. By Proposition 2.4, the existence of such a Massey product in H≤n+1(A)
obstructs n-formality. Therefore, A is not n-formal.
□

1-formality, as in the proof above.
In this case, Corollary 22.8 provides an independent obstruction when additionally [e] < im(µB)
(i.e., when [e] is not itself a cup product of degree-1 classes). In the typical Sasakian or Seifert case,
however, one has [e] ∈im(µB), so Corollary 22.8 does not apply, and the obstruction comes from
the Massey product argument.
For n > 1, neither obstruction applies: A is (n −1)-formal, hence 1-formal, and the obstruction
to formality lies at degree n rather than degree 1.
Corollary 22.13 ([88, 54]). Let M be a compact Sasakian manifold of dimension 2n + 1. Then M is
(n −1)-formal. If the Euler class of the characteristic S 1-bundle is nonzero, then M is not n-formal.
Proof. By Tievsky’s model, the minimal model of a compact Sasakian manifold M is a 1-step Hirsch
extension
(22.7)
M (M) = M (B) ⊗e
V(c),
where B is a compact K¨ahler orbifold of real dimension 2n. In particular, B is formal, its cohomol-
ogy is concentrated in degrees ≤2n, and it admits positive weights. The conclusion now follows
directly from Theorem 22.11.
□
Remark 22.14. Recall that the (2n+1)-dimensional Heisenberg nilmanifold M(n) is the quotient of
a certain nilpotent Lie group H(1, n) by the discrete, cocompact subgroup H(n). This nilmanifold is
a circle bundle over the compact complex torus E×n with Euler class equal to the K¨ahler form ω, and
thus it is a Sasakian manifold. In the reverse direction, the Tievsky model was used in [19, 54] to
show that a compact, (2n+1)-dimensional nilmanifold admits a Sasakian structure if and only if it is
the quotient of H(1, n) by a discrete, cocompact subgroup Γ. In particular, all Sasakian nilmanifolds
share the same rational homotopy type.
22.4. Malcev Lie algebras of Sasakian manifolds. Let M2n+1 be a compact Sasakian manifold,
with Reeb circle action S 1 →M
η−→B, where B is a compact K¨ahler orbifold of real dimension 2n.
As recalled in Section 22.3, M admits a finite-type cdga model of the form
A =  H∗(B; k) ⊗k
V(c), d,
d(c) = ω ∈H2(B; k),
where ω is the K¨ahler class of the base.
Since B is a compact K¨ahler orbifold, it is formal, and its cohomology algebra H∗(B; k) admits
positive weights. Equipping c with weight 1 when ω = 0, and with weight 2 when ω , 0, the
differential d is homogeneous with respect to these weights. Consequently, A is a minimal model
with positive Hirsch weights.
It follows from Proposition 18.8 that the fundamental group π1(M) is filtered-formal. In partic-
ular, its Malcev Lie algebra m(π1(M); k) is isomorphic to the degree completion of a graded Lie
algebra determined by the quadratic part of the differential in the minimal model.
To describe a presentation of the Malcev Lie algebra, let V = H1(B; k), and let h(B) denote the
holonomy Lie algebra of the base, i.e., h(B) = L(V)/⟨im(µ∨)⟩, where µ: V2 V →H2(B; k) is the
cup product. Let z be a generator in degree 2. The differential d(c) = ω induces a linear functional
ω∨: H2(B; k) →k, which corresponds, dually, to a central extension at the level of Lie algebras.

ALEXANDER I. SUCIU
More precisely, the Malcev Lie algebra of π1(M) is the degree completion of the graded Lie algebra
(22.8)
L(M) = L V∨ ⊕kz  D
im(µ∗), [x, y] = ω(x, y) z, [z, ·] = 0
E
,
where x, y ∈V and deg(z) = 2.
In other words, m(π1(M); k) is obtained from the holonomy Lie algebra of the base by adjoining a
central generator z in degree 2, with bracket determined by the K¨ahler form. When ω = 0, the exten-
sion is trivial and one recovers a direct product with kz. The presentation (22.8) makes transparent
the origin of non-graded-formality phenomena in Sasakian groups. While all Sasakian groups are
filtered-formal, the presence of a nontrivial central generator in degree 2 obstructs graded-formality
whenever ω , 0.
For the (2n+1)-dimensional Heisenberg nilmanifold M(n), the base is the complex torus B = E×n.
Thus, h(B) is the free abelian Lie algebra on 2n generators, and ω is a nondegenerate alternating
form. Formula (22.8) recovers the standard presentation of the Heisenberg Lie algebra, with z
corresponding to the central generator.
Remark 22.15. It is shown in [12] that all quadruple and higher order Massey products vanish
on any compact Sasakian manifold, even though such manifolds need not be formal. From the
perspective developed here, this phenomenon admits a natural explanation.
Indeed, the Tievsky model of a Sasakian manifold is a Hirsch extension of a formal cdga by a
single generator, endowed with positive weights. The associated Koszul spectral sequence there-
fore has no nontrivial differentials beyond d1. Since higher differentials dr, r ≥2, encode higher
order Massey products, this forces the vanishing of all Massey products of length ≥4. In this
sense, Sasakian manifolds exhibit the strongest possible failure of formality compatible with posi-
tive weights: triple Massey products may occur, but no higher order obstructions are allowed.
22.5. Seifert fibered spaces and Hirsch extensions. Let M be an orientable, closed Seifert fibered
spaces with orientable base. Such a 3-dimensional manifold admits an effective S 1-action with orbit
space an orientable surface Σg and finitely many exceptional orbits. The Seifert fibration η: M →Σg
is classified by integers (αi, βi) and an obstruction b = b(η).
The fundamental group Gη = π1(M) admits the presentation
(22.9)
⟨x1, y1, . . . , xg, yg, z1, . . . , zs, h | h central, [x1, y1] · · · [xg, yg]z1 · · · zs = hb, zαi
i hβi = 1⟩.
Assume g > 0. The minimal model of M is a Hirsch extension
(22.10)
M (M) ≃M (Σg) ⊗e
V(c),
where e ∈H2(Σg; k) represents the Euler class of the fibration. Since Σg is formal, this identifies
M (M) with a 1-step Hirsch extension of a formal cdga. Since Σg is formal and generated in degree
one, the Hirsch extension above admits positive Hirsch weights. By Proposition 18.8, the group
Gη is filtered-formal over k. The Malcev Lie algebra m(Gη) is therefore the degree completion of a
graded Lie algebra with generators in degree one and a single central generator in degree two when
e(η) , 0. This presentation, given explicitly in [110, Thm. 10.6], coincides with the specialization
of the Sasakian Malcev Lie algebra construction to the case B = Σg.
